- 2026-08-03 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c — refresh single-flight and 401 recovery wired into both
  Angular apps. Closes the `TODO(auth pass 3)` markers carried since Pass 2.**
  **What was actually broken.** A 401 dropped the session outright. That is worst on the
  admin, whose access cookie lives **15 minutes** against a 12-hour absolute window: an
  operator filling in a long form crossed that boundary routinely, and saving would fail and
  bounce them to login with the work on screen and unsavable. A reload after the access
  cookie lapsed also signed the user out even though the refresh cookie was valid for hours
  (admin) or months (storefront), because `/me` answered 401 and nothing tried to recover.
  **The recovery path.** A 401 on an ordinary request now rotates the session once and
  replays the request. Four rules make that safe rather than a new failure mode:
  (1) **One rotation, never one per request** — `RefreshCoordinator` from the transport
  package. The API's rotation is atomic and reuse-detecting, so a losing concurrent rotation
  presents a token that was just rotated away; the API cannot distinguish that from theft and
  revokes the whole refresh family. Since one expired access cookie fails EVERY in-flight
  request simultaneously, racing here is the normal case, not an edge case — the user would
  be signed out for the crime of loading two requests at once. (2) **Never on a credential
  endpoint** — a 401 from login/PIN/OTP/recovery means the credential was refused, and
  rotating after a failed rotation would be infinite. (3) **The replay re-reads the CSRF
  cookie** — rotation replaces `csrfSecretHash` server-side and issues a new `st_csrf`, so
  replaying with the pre-rotation header would 403 and read as an auth failure.
  (4) **One verdict per session** — a new opt-in `latchOnFailure` on the coordinator stops a
  failed rotation being re-attempted on every later 401, which bounds an anonymous visitor to
  a single refresh request rather than one per protected call. The latch clears when an
  authentication call succeeds, observed from the interceptor rather than by touching stores.
  Replaying the original request is safe because the API raises 401 in its global session
  guard BEFORE any handler runs, so the first attempt had no effect to duplicate. That is a
  different question from the package's `isRetryable`, which governs TRANSIENT retries and
  refuses unsafe methods precisely because those may have landed; the distinction is recorded
  in both interceptors so the two are not later "unified".
  **Where the knowledge lives.** The gateway gained `refreshSession()` and
  `isCredentialEndpoint(url)` — the classification belongs there because the gateway is the
  one file that owns route paths, and the interceptor asks rather than pattern-matching URLs
  it should not know. Matching is path-only, so a query string or the configured API origin
  cannot change the answer and a path that merely CONTAINS a credential route is not mistaken
  for one. A pre-check for a readable CSRF cookie skips rotation that could only 403, which
  also makes the storefront interceptor inert under SSR where there is no cookie jar.
  **Global error interceptor fixed in both apps (carried debt from Pass 2).** It logged and
  toasted every 401, including the anonymous `/me` probe that answers 401 by design — so
  every first page load looked broken. Worse after this pass: it sits downstream of the auth
  interceptor and would have put an error toast on screen during a refresh that then
  succeeded. 401 is now the auth layer's alone; an unrecoverable one is still visible because
  the session is cleared and the user returns to login.
  **Tests: repo 418 → 483.** http-transport 122 → **129** (latch semantics, including that
  joined callers still see the real rejection and that the default stays unlatched, so 3a's
  documented behaviour is unchanged). Admin 20 → **40**, storefront 16 → **37**: interceptor
  specs covering rotate-and-replay, concurrent-401 single flight, CSRF re-read, credential
  endpoints, rotation failure, the latch and its clearing, second-401 on replay, non-auth
  replay failure, and no-CSRF-cookie; plus a new gateway spec per app.
  **The gateway spec is worth describing.** It writes NO route literals. Each URL is captured
  from the gateway's own request through `provideHttpClientTesting` and fed back into
  `isCredentialEndpoint`, so the route table and the classification cannot drift apart — and
  no credential path appears outside the one HTTP adapter, which `check-browser-auth`
  enforces and specs are not exempt from.
  **Mutation-verified, three ways.** Bypassing the coordinator failed the concurrency test AND
  the latch test; replaying without re-preparing the request failed the CSRF re-read test;
  classifying `/me` as a credential endpoint failed the gateway spec. All reverted and
  md5-verified.
  **One stale-scaffold fix.** `apps/storefront/tsconfig.spec.json` declared `types:
["jasmine"]` — a leftover from the Angular scaffold describing a runner this repo does not
  use. It also meant a spec importing the app's runtime config failed the Analog transform
  with "Cannot find name 'process'" (the SSR branch's `process.env` lookup, which
  `tsconfig.app.json` already declares `node` for). Changed to `["node"]`.
  **Deliberately NOT wired: `toTransportFailure`.** The interceptors classify on
  `error.status`, and the package's error mapping stays unused — bundle-verified, its strings
  are absent from both apps. Nothing in the transport layer renders a message today; wiring
  it would be decoration, and the sub-code the admin UI actually needs to tell "wrong PIN"
  from "PIN locked" from "suspended pending revalidation" is an **API** gap (the global filter
  collapses every 401 to the generic envelope), still tracked as carried debt for pass 4/6.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm typecheck` 15/15 · `pnpm lint` 17/17 (0 errors) +
  `check:naming` + `check:browser-auth` both OK · `pnpm format:check` all files ·
  `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** · `git diff --check` clean · both Angular production
  builds · nothing staged. Bundle proof: the coordinator ships in the admin bundle, the
  storefront client bundle and `dist/ssr/main.server.js`; no `ZodError` in either client
  bundle. Adapter DB integration **not** re-run (no persistence touched).
  **No browser E2E — stated plainly.** Rotation is now unit-proven at the interceptor
  boundary, but the behaviour that matters (a real expired access cookie recovering against
  the live API without the refresh family being revoked) has not been exercised in a browser.
  For the storefront that is worth doing and is owed; for the admin it remains blocked on 6a,
  since no first-administrator bootstrap exists to sign in with.
  **Next executable step:** 3d — wire `@saha-textile/contracts` into both apps and replace the
  hand-written wire DTOs in the gateways, checking the bundle-size impact since contracts
  pulls Zod. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-03 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.1 — three defects in 3c's own recovery path closed,
  one of them a regression of the very bug 3c set out to fix.**
  **1. A returning visitor was still logged out (the serious one).** 3c refused to rotate
  when no CSRF cookie was readable, reasoning that the POST would 403. Checking that claim
  against the API showed the reasoning was right and the conclusion wrong: `csrfCookieOptions`
  in `apps/api/src/common/cookies.ts` sets **no `maxAge`**, so `st_csrf` is a browser-SESSION
  cookie, while `sessionCookieOptions` gives access and refresh an explicit one. Close the
  browser, come back tomorrow, and a storefront customer inside their 30-day refresh window —
  or an operator inside their 12-hour one — arrives with a perfectly valid session and no
  readable double-submit half. 3c dropped them for it. The interceptor now acquires a token
  through the gateway's existing `ensureCsrfToken()` and then rotates, both inside the
  coordinator so the acquisition is single-flighted too. The guard that remains is
  `hasBrowserCookieJar()` — new in the transport package — which excludes server rendering
  only, and for a different reason: there is no jar to populate and no user agent to carry
  the result, so the requests could never help rather than merely lacking a value.
  **2. Cross-tab rotation race (introduced by 3c).** Single-flight is per tab. Two tabs whose
  access cookies lapse together could both rotate; the loser presents a token that was just
  rotated away, which the API reads as theft — it revokes the whole family and records a
  "reuse detected" security event that never happened. Closed with the **Web Locks API**
  (owner-selected over a `BroadcastChannel` leader) via a new `withBrowserLock`. Reasons
  recorded because the choice is not obvious: this is mutual exclusion, not messaging, and
  the browser releases a lock automatically if the holding tab is closed mid-rotation —
  a hand-rolled leader needs a timeout for that, and every value is either short enough to
  permit the second rotation it was meant to prevent or long enough to hang every other tab.
  Where Web Locks is unavailable (pre-15.4 Safari, insecure context, SSR) it falls through to
  exactly the previous behaviour rather than refusing to rotate. A follower that waits and
  then rotates again is accepted rather than optimised away: the leader has already set a new
  cookie, so no reuse is detected. Lock names are audience-specific so a shared origin could
  not serialise two unrelated sessions.
  **3. Logout cleared the refresh latch.** The latch was reset on ANY 2xx from a credential
  endpoint, and logout is one — so after signing out, the next 401 spent a pointless rotation.
  The gateway gained `isSessionEstablishingEndpoint()`, a strict subset: logout succeeds by
  ENDING a session, `password/forgot` only sends an email, `password/reset` revokes every
  session by design, and an OTP REQUEST only issues a challenge — it is the verify that mints
  the session. Admin `resume` does belong there: it extends the operator's existing session,
  so its success is direct evidence that session is alive.
  **Plus the trailing fix from the same review:** `apps/admin/tsconfig.spec.json` carried the
  identical stale `types: ["jasmine"]` the storefront had, and `@types/jasmine` is **not
  installed anywhere in the workspace** — the entry named a package that does not exist. It
  had not bitten admin only because no admin spec had yet imported something needing Node
  types. Aligned to `["node"]`.
  **Tests: repo 483 → 512.** http-transport 129 → **142** (`withBrowserLock` incl. serialised
  holders, failure release, and every ambient-detection fallback; `hasBrowserCookieJar`
  including the distinction that an EMPTY jar is recoverable while no jar is not). Admin
  43 → **50**, storefront 40 → **48**: CSRF acquire-then-rotate, its failure path, both latch
  directions, the cross-tab lock (asserting the audience-specific name), and gateway coverage
  proving every session-establishing route is also a credential route while the reverse is
  not. **Mutation-verified three ways:** restoring the `readCsrfToken() !== null` guard failed
  both acquire-then-rotate cases; removing `withBrowserLock` failed the lock case; adding
  logout to the session-establishing list failed the gateway case. All reverted, md5-checked.
  **Two limits stated rather than papered over.** The SSR branch cannot be reproduced at the
  interceptor level — jsdom always provides a document — so it is covered in the package
  against `hasBrowserCookieJar()`, and both specs say so where the case would be expected.
  And a genuinely anonymous visitor now pays one CSRF acquisition plus one refused rotation on
  first load before the latch settles; that is the deliberate price of not logging out
  returning users, and it is bounded to one attempt per page load.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm typecheck` 15/15 · `pnpm lint` 17/17 (0 errors) +
  `check:naming` + `check:browser-auth` both OK · `pnpm format:check` all files ·
  `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** · `git diff --check` clean · both Angular production
  builds · nothing staged. Bundle proof: the audience-specific lock names appear in the admin
  bundle, the storefront client bundle and `dist/ssr/main.server.js`; no `ZodError` in either
  client bundle. Adapter DB integration **not** re-run (no persistence touched). Still no
  browser or live-API exercise of rotation — that is 3c.2.
  **Next executable step:** 3c.2 — a deterministic cookie-jar test against the real API with a
  short `JWT_ACCESS_TTL`: sign in, let the access cookie expire, hit a protected route, and
  assert rotation, replay, the CSRF-recovery path, and that the refresh family is NOT revoked.
  Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-03 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.2 — session rotation proven against the REAL API and a
  real replica set. 7/7 cases pass; the client half was already unit-proven, this is the
  server half.**
  **What it proves.** Registration issues `st_access`/`st_refresh`/`st_csrf` with no token
  material in the body; an access JWT that has genuinely expired makes `/me` answer 401 while
  the refresh cookie is still valid; rotating recovers it and the replayed request succeeds;
  three sequential rotations do **not** revoke the family (a false reuse detection would have
  signed the user out — the exact failure the client's single-flight and cross-tab lock exist
  to avoid); replaying a rotated-away token IS detected, with the family revoked and
  `revokeReason: 'reuse_detected'` persisted; and a session missing only its CSRF half
  recovers through `GET /auth/csrf` and then rotates.
  **Command:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` (builds the API first). It is
  deliberately outside `pnpm test`, like the adapter integration suite, because it needs a
  live replica set.
  **A production refactor was required first, and it is the interesting part.** `main.ts`
  held composition AND startup, so the only way to exercise the real pipeline was to run the
  real server — which is why no test ever had. Composition moved to `src/bootstrap.ts`
  (`createApp`), leaving `main.ts` with exactly what a test must not do: read the ambient
  environment and take a port. A harness that re-created the wiring itself would have been
  worse than none, because it would prove an application production does not run. Verified by
  booting the rebuilt API: `/health/live` 200, `/health/ready` 200 with `mongodb: up`,
  `/openapi.json` 200, correlation ids and redacted pino logging intact.
  **Why a script and not a Vitest suite — stated because it is a real constraint.** Nest
  resolves constructor dependencies from `emitDecoratorMetadata`, which esbuild (and so
  Vitest) cannot emit; booting `AppModule` under Vitest fails with `Nest can't resolve
dependencies of the SessionGuard (?, …)`. The options were `unplugin-swc` + `@swc/core` —
  two dependencies, one with a native binary, purely for test tooling — or driving the
  compiled output, which `nest build` produces through tsc with full metadata. The compiled
  output is also the artifact production runs, so it is the higher-fidelity option as well as
  the cheaper one.
  **Four things the run surfaced, three of them my own errors — recorded because the last one
  nearly became a false report.**
  (1) **A real trap in my own extraction:** `createApp(config)` shapes only the Fastify
  adapter, CORS and rate limits. `ConfigModule` provides `APP_CONFIG` through its own
  `loadConfig()` call, so every service reads `process.env` independently — the harness passed
  `JWT_ACCESS_TTL=1s` and watched the access cookie refuse to expire. The parameter now
  documents this loudly, and the harness sets `process.env`.
  (2) **Reuse detection is shadowed by fail-closed CSRF** when only the refresh cookie is
  presented: the guard cannot resolve a session for a rotated-away token, so the request is
  refused 403 before the handler runs. Isolating the stale refresh token as the ONLY stale
  value (keeping a valid access cookie and current CSRF) is what makes it a test of reuse
  detection rather than of CSRF.
  (3) **Registration is IP-rate-limited under the `login` action and answers 401, not 429**,
  so the response confirms nothing. Injected requests share one synthetic address and the
  counters persist, so a third consecutive run failed every case with a misleading
  "registration failed: 401". The harness now clears `authRateLimits` per run — harness
  hygiene, not a weakened control; the limiter has its own tests.
  (4) **A defect I nearly reported and did not.** The assertion on the revocation reason
  failed, and a case-sensitive grep for `reason` missed `revokeReason`, which led me toward
  "Mongoose silently strips an undeclared field". Probing the raw persisted document showed
  `revokeReason: "reuse_detected"` stored correctly — the field name in my assertion was
  simply wrong. No defect; the inference was.
  **Evidence captured for the pending 3c.3 decision.** The two refusal states ARE
  distinguishable: session cookies present with the CSRF half missing → **403**; no cookies at
  all → **401**. So the client can safely rotate FIRST and acquire a CSRF token only on a 403,
  which would cut an anonymous visitor's first-load cost from two requests to one while
  keeping the returning-visitor recovery intact. Recommended as 3c.3, now on evidence rather
  than assumption.
  **The SSR-wiring gap from 3c.1 is closed too.** That limit was stated generously: what was
  untested was not SSR behaviour but whether the interceptor CONSULTS the guard at all — jsdom
  always provides a jar, so deleting `canAttemptRefresh()` would have left every case green.
  Both specs now shadow `document.cookie` with a non-string getter, reproducing exactly the
  branch `hasBrowserCookieJar()` keys on. **Mutation-verified:** removing the guard now fails
  that case, where before it failed nothing. Admin 50 → **51**, storefront 48 → **49**.
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **7/7** ·
  `pnpm typecheck` 15/15 · `pnpm lint` 17/17 (0 errors) + `check:naming` + `check:browser-auth`
  · `pnpm format:check` all files · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** (repo 512 → 514) ·
  `MONGODB_PORT=27018 RUN_DB_IT=1` adapter IT **89/89** · both Angular production builds ·
  `git diff --check` clean · nothing staged. Probe accounts created 6, remaining after cleanup
  **0**, in a dedicated `saha_textile_e2e` database so a failed run cannot leave debris in the
  working one.
  **Still not done:** no browser E2E. Rotation is now proven at both the interceptor boundary
  and against the live API, but not through a real Angular app in a real browser; the
  storefront run is worth doing opportunistically and the admin one remains blocked on 6a.
  **Next executable step:** 3c.3 — reorder the client to rotate first and acquire a CSRF token
  only on the 403 this pass documented, then 3d (contracts in both apps). Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-04 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.2a — two follow-ups closed properly, one of them a real
  security gap.**
  **1. `createApp(config)` is now true end to end.** It previously shaped only the HTTP
  adapter: `ConfigModule` provided `APP_CONFIG` through its own `loadConfig()` call, so every
  service read `process.env` behind the caller's back. `ConfigModule.forRoot(config)` and
  `AppModule.forRoot(config)` now thread an explicit config through DI, defaulting to
  `loadConfig()` for process startup. The e2e harness passes `JWT_ACCESS_TTL` **only** as
  config, which is what makes the expiry case proof rather than decoration. **Mutation-verified:**
  restoring the internal `loadConfig()` fails "recovers an expired access cookie" — the exact
  symptom that exposed it. One documented exception remains: the Mongo adapter reads its
  connection settings from `process.env` at connect time, so redirecting the database still
  needs `MONGODB_DB_NAME` in the environment.
  **2. Refresh-token reuse detection no longer sits behind CSRF — the security fix.** Detection
  lived inside the refresh use case, which runs after `CsrfGuard`. An attacker holding a
  stolen refresh cookie and no CSRF token was refused with a 403 and the family was **never
  revoked**: blocked, but silent — the legitimate session kept working and no security event
  was recorded. Detection was effectively reachable only in the same-browser race. Extracted
  to `SessionService.revokeFamilyIfRefreshReused` and run by a new `RefreshReuseGuard`
  registered **before** `CsrfGuard`; `refresh()` still calls it so the use case is safe on its
  own terms. The guard is opt-in through `@RotatesSession()` because the check costs a
  database lookup — running it on every request carrying a session cookie would put it on the
  hot path of the whole API to protect two routes. It is not a new denial-of-service vector:
  a cross-site request carries whatever cookie the browser currently holds, which is the
  CURRENT token, and reaching the branch requires a 256-bit value matching a stored
  previous-token hash. **Mutation-verified:** moving the guard back behind `CsrfGuard` fails
  the new case and nothing else.
  **Tests:** e2e 7 → **8** (a stolen refresh cookie with no CSRF token now revokes the family
  and the victim's session is gone, asserted against the database rather than a status code);
  api 79 → **83** (`refresh-reuse.guard.test.ts`, including that a non-rotating handler
  performs no lookup). Repo 514 → **518**.
  **Two items reported rather than changed.** `revokeReason` needed nothing: it is declared on
  the schema, mapped by the adapter and typed in contracts as `SessionRevokeReason` — the
  earlier suspicion came from a case-sensitive grep and was wrong, and the e2e now asserts it.
  Rate-limit bucket sharing is a tuning decision left for the owner: registration, storefront
  login and admin login all consume one `login` bucket keyed by IP at 10 per 15 minutes, and
  admin PIN login uses 5, so a NAT-shared address can lock out unrelated users across
  audiences — and the refusal is a deliberate 401 that reads as "invalid credentials".
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **8/8** ·
  `pnpm typecheck` 15/15 · `pnpm lint` 17/17 + `check:naming` + `check:browser-auth` ·
  `pnpm format:check` all files · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** ·
  `MONGODB_PORT=27018 RUN_DB_IT=1` adapter IT **89/89** · real API boot re-probed after the DI
  change (`/health/live` 200, `/health/ready` 200 with `mongodb: up`, `/openapi.json` 200) ·
  probe accounts 7, remaining after cleanup **0**.
  **Next executable step:** 3c.3 — rotate first and acquire a CSRF token only on the 403 the
  harness documented, then a storefront browser E2E. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-04 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.2b — authentication rate limiting rebuilt around
  carrier-grade NAT. Owner-approved: failure counting, separate buckets, policy in core.**
  **The problem was the counting model, not the ceiling.** `withinRateLimit` consumed a slot
  BEFORE credentials were verified, so every successful login spent budget. Indian consumer
  internet is overwhelmingly CGNAT — Jio and Airtel put thousands of subscribers behind one
  public IPv4 — so ten honest logins from one carrier address locked out everyone else behind
  it, and the refusal was a deliberate 401 that reads as "invalid credentials". Raising the
  number would only have moved the cliff while weakening the control against real attackers.
  **Failure counting inverts it.** An attacker's requests are all failures and all counted; a
  success costs nothing and clears that account's budget. Actions that spend a real resource
  on success — registration, OTP request, password reset — cannot use it, because the cost
  lands whether or not the caller was honest, so those still count attempts and lean on the
  per-identifier scope to stay fair. **The shared address budget is deliberately never cleared
  on success:** an attacker could otherwise wipe accumulated failures by logging into an
  account they control.
  **Separate buckets.** Storefront login, admin login, registration, PIN login, OTP request,
  OTP verify and password reset each have their own. They shared one `login` bucket, so a
  burst of customer traffic could exhaust the budget the back office depends on — staff locked
  out of admin by shoppers on the same office address.
  **The PIN ceiling was the sharpest hazard.** The owner lock is five failed attempts locking
  the PIN for fifteen minutes, and that is per ACCOUNT through `pinLockedUntil`. A _per-address_
  ceiling of five was a different control wearing the same number: any five failures behind a
  carrier address locked PIN login for every operator on it. The address ceiling is now 200 and
  the account lock is untouched.
  **Hexagonal, as asked.** Policy moved to `packages/core-domain/src/auth`: pure data
  (`AUTH_RATE_LIMIT_POLICY`) plus a pure decision (`decideRateLimit`), with no clock, storage
  or framework. The port stays the counter store, so Mongo → Redis later changes no number.
  `peek(key, now)` was added to `AuthRateLimitRepository` because the refusal decision has to
  be made before a credential check while the counter may only advance after it fails; the
  adapter re-checks window expiry in code because TTL deletion is asynchronous.
  **IPv6 is keyed by its `/64`.** A subscriber owns a whole /64 while privacy extensions rotate
  the low bits, often hourly — so counting the full address would let one handset shed a limit
  by ageing its address, and counting a shared IPv4 punishes strangers. Since Indian mobile
  traffic is heavily IPv6, the address scope is now meaningfully accurate for most real users.
  Pure string handling, no `node:net`, and an unparseable address falls back to its trimmed
  form so malformed input cannot collapse into one shared bucket.
  **Response semantics.** Address-scoped refusals answer **429 with `Retry-After`** — the limit
  is a property of the network, not of any account, so it reveals nothing and gives an honest
  user something actionable. Identifier-scoped refusals keep the generic 401 so an attacker
  cannot read the response to pace themselves, and the anti-enumeration endpoints (OTP request,
  both password-recovery requests) still answer with their generic accepted body either way.
  **Numbers, all in one reviewable table:** storefront login 10/IP 100 per 15 min · admin login
  10/IP 50 · admin PIN IP 200 (account lock unchanged at 5) · OTP verify 10/IP 100 · register
  3 per address per hour/IP 30 · OTP request 5 per 15 min/IP 60 per hour · password reset 5/IP
  30 per hour. Deliberately not environment variables: these are policy, not deployment
  topology, and should not differ per environment.
  **Tests.** core-domain 36 → **87** (decision boundaries, that the address ceiling is always
  looser than the identifier one, that credential actions count failures while resource-spending
  actions count attempts, and 30 address-scope cases including IPv4-mapped, zone identifiers,
  and malformed input that must never throw inside an auth path). e2e 8 → **10**.
  **Mutation-verified twice, and the first attempt was a bad test.** The e2e case originally
  drove fourteen logins at a ceiling of 100 — it would have passed under attempt counting too,
  proving the ceiling rather than the model. Rewritten to assert the COUNTERS: successful logins
  leave zero rows, and after four failures a success clears the identifier row while the address
  row stays at four. Reverting to `consumeRateLimit` fails both; also resetting the address
  budget on success fails the second alone.
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **10/10** ·
  `pnpm typecheck` 15/15 · `pnpm lint` 17/17 + `check:naming` + `check:browser-auth` ·
  `pnpm format:check` all files · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** (contracts 145,
  http-transport 142, core-domain 87, api 83, storefront 49, admin 51) ·
  `MONGODB_PORT=27018 RUN_DB_IT=1` adapter IT **89/89**. Per-package counts are quoted from
  here on: the running repo total was ambiguous about the DB-gated suite, which reports 4 or 89
  depending on whether it is enabled.
  **One papercut fixed in passing:** `pnpm e2e:session-rotation` built only the API, so a
  changed adapter left a stale `dist` and the run failed with `this.rateLimits.peek is not a
function`. It now builds through Turbo so dependencies rebuild with it.
  **Next executable step:** 3c.3 — rotate first and acquire a CSRF token only on the 403 the
  harness documented, then a storefront browser E2E. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-04 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.3 — the client asks before it fetches: rotate first,
  acquire a CSRF token only on a 403.**
  **What it costs today, and why it changed.** 3c.1 fixed the returning-visitor logout by
  acquiring a CSRF token whenever none was readable, then rotating. Correct, but it charged
  the cost to the wrong people: every anonymous first page load fired `/me` (401) →
  `/auth/csrf` (200) → `refresh` (401), fetching a token for a session the visitor does not
  have. On a storefront that is two avoidable round-trips on first paint for every SEO visitor.
  **The reorder is safe because the API's refusals are distinguishable**, which 3c.2 asserted
  rather than assumed: session cookies present with the CSRF half missing answers **403**, no
  session at all answers **401**. So the client now rotates first; on 403 it fetches a token
  and retries once, and on 401 it gives up and latches. An anonymous load costs ONE request
  instead of two; a returning visitor pays three and still gets their session back. Both paths
  stay inside the coordinator, so the whole sequence is single-flighted, and there is no
  recursion risk because the refresh route is a credential endpoint whose own failures never
  re-enter recovery.
  **Tests:** admin 51 → **52**, storefront 49 → **50**. The two specs that encoded the old
  order were rewritten rather than deleted, and a third case added for the saving itself — a
  401 refusal must fetch NO token. **Mutation-verified:** restoring acquire-first fails all
  three and nothing else.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm typecheck` 15/15 · `pnpm lint` 17/17 + `check:naming` +
  `check:browser-auth` · `pnpm format:check` all files · `pnpm turbo run test --force` 11/11
  (contracts 145, http-transport 142, core-domain 87, api 83, storefront 50, admin 52) ·
  `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **10/10** · both Angular production builds ·
  `git diff --check` clean.
  **Next executable step:** the storefront browser E2E — specifically the four things nothing
  else can show: that `st_csrf` really is dropped on browser close while `st_refresh` survives,
  the Web Locks path across two real tabs, recovery inside the real Angular DI and RxJS chain,
  and SSR not attempting recovery. Admin browser E2E stays blocked on 6a. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-05 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.4 — storefront browser E2E run. All four browser-only
  claims verified against a real dev server and a real API; the admin run is no longer
  described as blocked.**
  **Correction first.** Earlier entries said admin browser E2E was blocked on 6a because "no
  admin account can exist to sign in with". That was overstated: the continuation brief's
  gotcha 10 explicitly sanctions seeding an admin row directly for probing, and passes 2b/2c
  already did exactly that for the API probes. What 6a actually blocks is the **invite →
  accept → first login journey**, not signing in. The admin run was deferred, not prevented.
  **Setup:** API rebuilt and run with `JWT_ACCESS_TTL=30s` against rs0 on 27018, storefront
  dev server on 4200 through `.claude/launch.json`, real registration through the UI form.
  **1. The returning-visitor premise, confirmed from the wire.** `curl -D -` on register shows
  `st_access` and `st_refresh` with `Max-Age=2591999; HttpOnly`, and **`st_csrf` with no
  `Max-Age` and no `Expires`** — a browser-session cookie by definition. That is the whole
  basis of the 3c.1 fix, and until now it had only been read off `csrfCookieOptions` in source
  rather than observed. In the browser, `document.cookie` exposed ONLY `st_csrf`; storage held
  `language`, `ngrx_cart`, `site_config_session` and nothing session-shaped.
  **2. Expired-access recovery, in the real Angular runtime.** After the 30-second access
  cookie lapsed, a reload produced `/me` **401** → `refresh` **200** → `/me` **200**, and the
  browser stayed on `/account/dashboard` signed in. Before pass 3c this was a logout.
  **3. The 3c.3 order, observed end to end.** Dropping `st_csrf` (what a browser restart does)
  and reloading produced exactly the designed sequence: `/me` 401 → `refresh` **403** →
  `/auth/csrf` 200 → `refresh` **200** → `/me` **200**. And for an anonymous visitor the
  bootstrap is `/me` 401 → `refresh` 401 and then nothing — one recovery request, not two, which
  is the saving 3c.3 exists for. (`/auth/csrf` also appears on the register page; that is the
  page priming its own token, not recovery.)
  **4. Web Locks proven across two real tabs, with timestamps.** `navigator.locks` is present
  (`isSecureContext` true on localhost), so the real path is taken rather than the fallback —
  and the fallback would be indistinguishable without forcing contention, so contention was
  forced. A second tab held `saha-textile-storefront-session-refresh` from 1785879431580 to
  1785879451847 while the first tab's session lapsed. The first tab issued `/me` 401 at
  1785879441860 and its rotation at **1785879451857 — 10 ms after the lock released**, having
  waited a full ten seconds. That is the cross-tab mutual exclusion working, not inferred.
  **5. SSR does not attempt recovery.** A server-side fetch of `/account/dashboard` returned
  200 with 72 KB of HTML containing no user data, and the API's authenticated-request count was
  **unchanged across it (42 → 42)** — no `/me`, no rotation. jsdom cannot reproduce this branch,
  which is why it was owed.
  **Hygiene:** two probe accounts created (one through the UI, one by curl for the cookie
  attributes) and both deleted with their sessions and verification tokens; `users` and
  `authSessions` back to **0**; rate-limit counters cleared. An API log scan found **zero**
  occurrences of the probe password, a refresh cookie value or a JWT.
  **Honest limits.** A true browser restart is not reproducible in this pane, so the cookie
  premise was established from the `Set-Cookie` attributes and the effect reproduced by clearing
  that one cookie. Two tabs recovering simultaneously by chance was not forced either — the lock
  was held deliberately, which is a stronger test than a race that may not occur.
  **Next executable step:** admin browser E2E against a directly-seeded admin row (sanctioned by
  gotcha 10, deleted afterwards with a count check), covering password and PIN login, the
  15-minute idle window and rotation mid-form. The invite → accept journey stays with 6a.
  Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-06 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 3c.5 — admin browser E2E run, after finding and fixing an
  infinite router redirect loop that made the whole back office unusable.**
  **The defect.** `app.routes.ts` applied `AuthGuard` as `canActivateChild` to the **`auth`**
  routes and to nothing else — exactly inverted. An anonymous visit to `/auth/login` hit the
  guard, which answers `createUrlTree(['/auth/login'])`, whose target is a child of the same
  guarded route, which ran the guard again: the router span the main thread at **100.3% CPU**
  on a blank page, with no console, no tab close and no further network traffic. `path: ''`
  redirects to `auth/login`, so every entry point landed in it. The back office shells
  (`content`, `full`) meanwhile carried **no guard at all**, so they were reachable while
  signed out — presentation only, since every admin endpoint enforces audience, role and
  permissions server-side, but an operator got an empty frame answering 401 rather than a
  login screen.
  **Why it surfaced only now, which is the part worth remembering.** It was masked by the
  `FAKE_TOKEN` the admin used to seed into `localStorage`: `isAuthenticated()` answered true
  before anyone signed in, so the guard always passed and never looped. Removing that token in
  the cookie-session migration (Pass 2) is what exposed a misplacement that arrived with the
  original template port in `f9233404`. The auth work did not cause it; it revealed it.
  **Fix:** guard removed from `auth`, applied to both back-office shells, with
  `app.routes.spec.ts` pinning both halves — the authentication screens must stay reachable
  while signed out, and every shell with children must be guarded. Admin 52 → **54**.
  **Diagnosis is worth recording because two hypotheses were wrong first.** A blank page plus
  a dead console read as a rendering fault; it was a spin. The owner's Chrome Task Manager
  reading (100.3% CPU, 27 MB) is what separated "infinite loop" from "awaiting forever" and
  turned guesswork into a search. Before that I removed a `router.navigate` from the admin
  interceptor on a startup-deadlock theory and **disproved it myself** with a TestBed
  reproduction: when every request is answered the way the real API answers it, `bootstrap()`
  settles cleanly. That change is kept as a cleanup on its own merits — an interceptor should
  not drive routing when the guard already does it declaratively — and is labelled as such,
  not as the fix.
  **Every browser-only claim now verified on the admin surface.** Login screen renders;
  **no token in `localStorage`** (only `setting_store`) and only `st_csrf` readable, which is
  the historic admin defect confirmed gone in a real browser; password login reaches
  `/dashboard`; a reload survives from cookies alone (`me` 200, no rotation); an expired access
  cookie recovers as `me` 401 → `refresh` 200 → `me` 200 with the operator still on
  `/dashboard`; dropping `st_csrf` reproduces the returning-visitor path exactly —
  `me` 401 → `refresh` **403** → `csrf` → `refresh` **200** → `me` 200; after logout,
  `/dashboard` redirects to a rendered login screen; and **PIN login** through
  `/auth/admin/login/pin` reaches `/dashboard` with storage still clean.
  **Honest limits.** Logout was performed through the endpoint from the page rather than the
  menu button: synthetic clicks did not reach the anchor's handler inside its dropdown, which
  is a harness limitation and not evidence of a defect. The 15-minute idle soft lock is not
  built yet (pass 6c), so it was not exercised. The invite → accept → first-login journey
  remains blocked on 6a.
  **Also fixed in passing:** `pnpm e2e:session-rotation` built only the API, leaving a changed
  adapter behind a stale `dist`; it now builds through Turbo. And a note on gate reporting —
  earlier "typecheck 15/15" lines were partly Turbo cache hits, one of them stale; wiping
  `.turbo` exposed a pre-existing `developer-portal-storybook` typecheck failure whose tsconfig
  maps `@core/*` to storefront before admin while typechecking the admin's `auth.store.ts`.
  Portal tooling, out of scope here, recorded rather than silently fixed.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** (contracts 145,
  http-transport 142, core-domain 87, api 83, storefront 50, admin 54) · admin production
  build · Prettier clean on every touched file · probe admin and its 3 sessions deleted with
  `users` and `authSessions` back to **0** · API log scan found **zero** occurrences of the
  probe password, the PIN or a refresh cookie.
  **Next executable step:** pass 4 — route/contract families, retire the dead legacy guards,
  stable OpenAPI tags and operation IDs, and the 401 sub-code for session-bound surfaces only.
  Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-07 — [DONE] **Admin login UI — PIN entered on an on-screen keypad, and the method
  toggle matched to the theme.** Owner-reported, outside the numbered auth passes.
  **The toggle.** "Use your PIN instead" was a `btn btn-link`, so Bootstrap gave it the blue
  underlined-hyperlink treatment beside the theme-styled "Forgot Password?". It now uses the
  theme's own `.forgot-password` on an `a[role=button]` with Enter/Space handlers, since it
  acts on the page rather than navigating. Verified by computed style rather than by eye: all
  **14** text properties are identical to "Forgot Password?" — `Public Sans` 16px/500,
  `letter-spacing: 1px`, `line-height: 24px`, `text-decoration: none`, `rgb(13, 164, 135)`.
  No new CSS.
  **The keypad (`shared/ui/pin-pad`).** There is **no `<input>` behind it** — that is the
  mechanism, not a detail. Nothing is focusable, so no device keyboard, native or third-party,
  browser or installed PWA, can be summoned on any platform. That is stronger than
  `inputmode="numeric"`, which only _asks_ for a numeric layout and is routinely ignored, and
  stronger than `readonly`, which suppresses the keyboard on some platforms and not others. It
  also keeps the digits out of a DOM input's `value`, so they cannot be autofilled,
  spell-checked or restored by form-restoration on back-navigation. This is the owner's stated
  preference — on-screen pad everywhere, device keyboard disabled outright — chosen over
  hiding the pad on small viewports. Digits, Backspace and Escape also work from a physical
  keyboard and produce the SAME visual press as a tap. Placed in `shared/ui` because 6b
  (Security Settings PIN change) and 6c (idle soft lock) will need it.
  **Three corrections after the owner looked at it, all real.** (1) The slot left of `0` was
  blank; it is now **Clear**, wiping the whole entry, with Escape as its keyboard equivalent.
  (2) Empty cells rendered no text, so they had **no line box** — every cell jumped from 18px
  to 47px the moment the first digit landed. The dot is now always in the markup and empty
  cells simply do not paint it, holding all six at **47×44** whether filled or not. (3) The
  keys had acquired a border: the theme's `.btn-outline` carries one, `.btn-outline-secondary`
  does not. Reverted to borderless.
  **Two theme facts worth recording, both found by measuring, not by guessing.**
  `.btn-outline-secondary` renders **borderless** here, so the first version looked like bare
  numerals rather than buttons. And this theme paints `.btn:disabled` as a **solid green fill
  with white text for every variant, plain `.btn` included** — which is what made the delete
  key a green blob. No keypad key is `disabled` any more; unavailable keys carry
  `aria-disabled` and 0.4 opacity, and every handler guards itself. The pressed state is a
  neutral wash plus `scale(0.96)` and an inset shadow rather than a colour change, because the
  theme sets `.btn` colours with `!important`; reduced-motion keeps the wash and drops the
  movement.
  **Verified in a real browser at desktop, 768 and 375.** Cells constant at 47×44 across
  empty/1-digit/3-digit; `inputsOnPage` is `["Email or username"]` at every viewport, so no
  PIN field exists to summon a keyboard; tap and physical keypress produce the same flash
  inside the window and clear after it; typing in the identifier does **not** leak into the
  PIN; Clear empties from both pointer and Escape and goes inert at 0.4 opacity when empty; no
  horizontal overflow. Re-verified after Prettier reflowed the cell markup, since that changed
  the whitespace around the dot.
  **Also:** added `pin-pad.stories.ts` (required by `check-component-coverage.mjs`) and the
  four new strings to **both** `en.json` and `fr.json` — fr was already missing 19 keys before
  this, a pre-existing gap left alone.
  **Commands actually run:** admin tests **54/54** · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11**
  (contracts 145, core-domain 87, http-transport 142, api 83, storefront 50, admin 54) ·
  admin production build · `pnpm format:check` clean · `pnpm --filter admin lint` **0 errors**
  (63 pre-existing warnings). The `developer-portal-storybook` `@core/*` alias typecheck
  failure recorded under 3c.5 is unchanged and still out of scope.
  **Next executable step:** unchanged — pass 4. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Admin translation catalogue brought back in step, and guarded.**
  Owner-requested after the keypad pass flagged the gap.
  **Why nothing had caught it.** Transloco runs with `fallbackLang: 'en'` and
  `missingHandler: { useFallbackTranslation: true }`, so a missing French string renders the
  English one. Excellent at runtime, invisible at build time — the gap could not be seen from
  the screen or from the tests, which is precisely the shape of rule that belongs in a lint
  guard.
  **What the audit found, beyond the 17 absent keys.** Two keys could never resolve **at all**
  because catalogue and template disagreed on case: `en` held `Setup` while the product form
  asks for `setup`, and `fr` held `Message` while the payout modal asks for `message` — so
  English showed a raw key on the product form and French did on the payout modal. Five
  **Hungarian** strings sat in `fr.json` (`Termékek`, `Minden felhasználó`), vendor-theme
  residue. Ten strings were never translated. Eight were the wrong sense of an English
  homograph — `Ordres` for orders (a purchase is a `commande`), `Taper` for type (to strike a
  key), `Lester` for weight (to add ballast), `Dos` for back (the body part), plus the typo
  `Evoyer`. Two zero-width spaces hid inside `Prix de vente`. `help_center` in the **English**
  catalogue read `Centre d'aide`. And `setup` was translated `Installation` though it labels a
  product-form tab, not an install step.
  **Also removed the dead `pin` key**, left from the FormControl the keypad replaced.
  `fr.json` is rebuilt in `en.json`'s key order so the two stay line-comparable: **805 / 805**.
  **The guard is the durable part.** `scripts/check-i18n-parity.mjs` holds key parity, case
  collisions, placeholder preservation, wrong-language residue, zero-width characters and
  untranslated strings, with a `SHARED_WORDS` allowance so legitimately identical values
  ("Blog", "Total", "PayPal") do not cry wolf. **Verified it fails on all five regression
  classes** before trusting it, then restored.
  **One ordering change worth naming.** The three guards now run BEFORE `turbo run lint`
  rather than after. They are fast and always correct, and chaining them behind a `turbo run
lint` that is red for an unrelated pre-existing reason meant they never executed at all.
  **An attempted fix that was reverted, recorded because the diagnosis matters.** The
  `developer-portal-storybook` `@core/*` failure is NOT alias ordering. Mapping
  `@core/auth/auth-gateway` explicitly at the admin merely moved the identical failure to the
  storefront: both apps export different symbols from the same specifier, so one flat tsconfig
  cannot satisfy both. It needs per-app tsconfigs or project references. Reverted rather than
  left half-fixed; still portal tooling and still out of scope.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test` **11/11** · all three guards OK ·
  `pnpm format:check` clean · French login and keypad screens driven in a real browser through
  Transloco (`Bienvenue sur Fastkart`, `Utiliser plutôt votre code PIN`, keypad `Effacer` /
  `Supprimer le dernier chiffre`, live region `2 chiffres du code PIN sur 6 saisis`).
  Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 4a — the dead legacy bearer guards retired, and their
  behaviour pinned shut.**
  **What went.** `jwt-auth.guard.ts`, `roles.guard.ts`, `roles.decorator.ts` and
  `current-user.decorator.ts` — verified beforehand as a **closed cluster**: zero references
  from anywhere outside, the only remaining edge being `roles.guard` importing `roles.decorator`,
  i.e. each other. Not barrelled, no specs. `AuthPort.verifyToken` survives the deletion, as
  `session.guard` and `session.service` are its real callers.
  **Why it mattered more than tidiness.** `JwtAuthGuard` accepted `Authorization: Bearer`. The
  locked model is API-set `httpOnly` cookie sessions; a bearer path reinstated by a future
  template port or a copied snippet would sidestep the CSRF double-submit entirely.
  **`ROLES_KEY_V2` is now `ROLES_KEY`.** The `_V2` suffix existed only to dodge a collision
  with the file just deleted, so the four metadata keys finally read consistently
  (`auth:public`, `auth:audience`, `auth:roles`, `auth:permissions`).
  **Deletion proves nothing on its own, so the behaviour is now pinned.** A new e2e check
  registers a customer, confirms the access token works as a cookie (**200**, so the token is
  unquestionably valid), then replays the SAME token at the same instant as a bearer header
  with no cookies and requires **401**. **Negative control run:** flipping the expectation to
  200 fails with `NEGATIVE CONTROL: 401`, so the check is not vacuous and would catch a
  regression.

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 4b — the 401 envelope can finally say WHY, on session-bound
  surfaces only.** Closes the API gap carried since Pass 2 (see "Known gaps" above).
  **The finding that shaped the pass, stated plainly.** The gap was originally written down as
  "the admin UI needs to tell wrong PIN from PIN locked from suspended". That specific fix
  **cannot be delivered at the login screen**, and building it would have been a security
  regression. `/auth/admin/login/pin` reaches its `locked` and `revalidation_required`
  outcomes only for an identifier that resolves to a real, active, non-customer account. So an
  attacker who sends five wrong PINs at a guessed identifier would read `pin_locked` for a
  genuine administrator and `invalid` for a stranger — a working account-enumeration oracle,
  which today's uniformly generic 401 does not give them. The lock state is safe to surface
  only once the operator has authenticated some other way (password login succeeding, then
  being told the PIN is locked), which is a session-bound surface and belongs to 6b/6c.
  **What shipped instead, and why it is the useful half.** `AuthRefusalReason` on the
  `ApiError` envelope, optional and absent by default, with five values that all describe the
  CALLER'S OWN session — proven by their own cookie, revealing nothing about anyone else:
  `session_missing`, `session_expired`, `session_revoked`, `permissions_changed`,
  `account_inactive`. The distinction a client actually needs is "expired, so rotate" versus
  "revoked, so stop asking"; collapsing those either signs people out mid-session or spins an
  anonymous visitor through one refresh per protected call.
  **The mechanism is a type, not a convention.** A new `SessionRefusal extends
UnauthorizedException` carries the reason, and the global filter reads it from the exception
  TYPE — never inferred from status, message or payload. A credential endpoint raises a plain
  `UnauthorizedException` and therefore cannot acquire a reason however its message is worded
  or wherever it is copied from. All eight `SessionGuard` refusal sites were converted.
  **The audience boundary was preserved deliberately.** A foreign-audience cookie answers
  `session_missing` — byte-identical to presenting no cookie — because a distinct reason there
  would rebuild the exact cross-audience probe the check exists to prevent. Pinned by a test
  asserting the two are equal, not merely that each is right.
  **One wrong expectation of mine, corrected by the run.** The logout tab does NOT read as
  `session_revoked`: logout clears the cookies, so it presents nothing and correctly reads

  **The rule added, as rule 7 in both interceptors.** A session-bound 401 may name a reason;
  `session_revoked` and `account_inactive` describe states rotation provably cannot fix,
  because the act that revoked the session invalidated the refresh token with it. Those skip
  the rotation round-trip and end the session at once.
  **The skip list is deliberately as short as the evidence allows, and `session_missing` is
  NOT on it** even though "no session, nothing to refresh" reads like it should be. The client
  cannot check whether a refresh cookie survived — it is `httpOnly` — and the costs are
  asymmetric: a needless rotation is ONE request, already bounded per session by the
  coordinator's failure latch, whereas wrongly skipping signs out somebody who would have been
  recovered. `permissions_changed` also keeps rotating, because rotation is precisely what
  mints a token carrying the new `permissionsVersion`.
  **Absence of a reason is never read as a verdict.** A refusal with no reason, or one naming
  a value this client does not recognise, takes the pre-4b path unchanged. That is deliberate
  forward-compatibility: a deployed client will meet an API that has grown a new reason, and
  an unknown string must not fall through a policy check as
  neither-recoverable-nor-unrecoverable.

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5a — canonical permission registry with stable codes.**
  Owner chose the scope after a sequencing hazard was surfaced.
  **The hazard, raised BEFORE writing anything.** `SessionGuard` holds that grants are
  authoritative and a role implies nothing ("An admin role does not imply every permission").
  So the moment any live route gains `@RequirePermissions`, every existing administrator —
  whose `permissions` array is empty — receives 403. With **6a** (first-admin bootstrap) and
  **5c** (grant surface) both unbuilt, nothing can grant a permission, so enforcing now would
  lock the back office with no route back in. Owner picked registry + grant validation, no
  route enforcement; deny-by-default lands in 5c with its escalation tests.
  **The codes are reconciled, not invented.** The admin application's navigation already gated
  on a permission vocabulary the API had never heard of: **26 codes across 22 resources**
  (`user.index`, `product.create`, `order.index`…). The registry is exactly that set. The API
  meanwhile accepted `z.array(z.string())` everywhere, so an invite could grant
  `prodcut.index` and the system would store it and authorize nothing.
  **What now holds.** `PermissionCode` is a closed Zod enum in `packages/contracts`;
  `PermissionGrant` validates a grant and NORMALIZES it (duplicates collapsed, sorted) so two
  equivalent grants are byte-identical and an audit diff shows a real change rather than a
  reordering; `AdminInviteRequest.permissions` uses it, so a mistyped code is refused at the
  boundary; and `RequirePermissions` is typed to `PermissionCode`, so a mistyped code now
  fails to COMPILE. Rejection is all-or-nothing per grant — partial acceptance would leave a
  caller believing they granted three permissions while the account held two.
  **Strict on the way in, permissive on the way out — deliberately.** The persisted
  `AdminInvite.permissions` and `UserAuthState.permissions` keep `string[]`. Tightening those
  would turn a row written before this registry existed into an unreadable document.
  **Only `index` and `create` exist**, because only those are in use. No speculative
  `product.update`/`destroy`: the routes needing them are not built, and 22 resources × 4
  actions would make the registry mostly untested fiction. Adding an action is a deliberate
  edit plus the route that consumes it, which is what "stable codes" should cost.
  **`scripts/check-permission-registry.mjs` keeps the two lists identical in both directions**
  — a menu code the server cannot grant means an invisible link forever; a registry code no
  screen uses means a grant that authorizes nothing. It runs in `pnpm lint` (source-level, no
  build) and needs no dependency between the packages, which is necessary because the admin
  app does not consume contracts (3d is unbuilt).
  **A defect in my own guard, found by negative-controlling it.** The first version only
  counted menu strings whose resource AND action were already declared — which quietly
  defeated the rule it existed for: a navigation entry gating on `user.destroy` was discarded
  as "not a permission" instead of reported as one the server cannot grant. **The check failed
  to fail.** Filtering by what is already known can only confirm what is already known. The
  parser now takes every `resource.action`-shaped string, which is safe here because icons and
  route paths do not match the shape and all 26 dotted strings in that file are permissions.
  **7/7 rules proven to fail on violation** after the fix (menu code absent from registry,
  menu code with an unknown resource, registry code no screen uses, unsorted registry,
  undeclared resource, orphan resource, duplicate code), each restored and the menu verified
  unchanged by `git diff`.

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5b — the `roles` and `userRoleAssignments` collections, their
  repositories, and the unique ACTIVE assignment index.** Both were Schema Nebula nodes marked
  `planned` / `add`; both now exist.
  **Built to the locked node definitions, not invented.** `roles` — "Reusable role definitions
  and permission groupings for administrative authorization"; `userRoleAssignments` —
  "Explicit user-to-role assignments with lifecycle and auditability", referencing `users` and
  `roles`. Registered in `COLLECTION_NAMES`, so the physical names are reviewed rather than
  left to Mongoose.
  **Why roles are a collection when `UserRole` already exists.** The enum is the COARSE tier
  and stays exactly as it is — it decides which audience a session may serve and is baked into
  an access token. The collection is the fine-grained half: named, reusable permission bundles
  an operator can define without a deployment. They are not alternatives, and a role carries a
  `baseRole` so it can never grant an audience its own tier does not allow.
  **The constraint this pass exists for.** A user may hold a role once at a time, and may hold
  it again after revocation. A plain unique index on `(userId, roleId)` enforces the first
  half and permanently breaks the second, because revocation keeps the row — re-granting would
  collide with a record that no longer confers anything. It is therefore a PARTIAL unique
  index over `revokedAt: null`, verified present in the live database as
  `unique: true, partialFilterExpression: { revokedAt: null }`.
  **Enforced by the database on purpose.** Two concurrent grants both read "no active
  assignment" and both insert; no application check can arbitrate that. The adapter translates
  MongoDB's 11000 into a named `AssignmentAlreadyActiveError`, so callers can tell "already
  granted" from "the write failed" without reading driver error codes.
  **Assignments are never hard-deleted**, which is what makes "who held this authority, and
  when" answerable. Revocation is idempotent — the `revokedAt: null` filter means a second
  revoke returns null rather than rewriting when the first one truly happened.
  **12 new integration tests against the real rs0**, adapter **89 → 100**: round-trip, unique
  key, mutable-half update, system roles refusing update and delete, grant/active listing,
  named duplicate error, re-grant after revoke, idempotent revoke, two concurrent grants where
  exactly one wins, and cross-user isolation. Probe rows verified **0 remaining**.
  **Negative-controlled the constraint itself.** Dropping the partial filter fails exactly one
  test — "allows re-granting a role after it was revoked" — and nothing else, so the suite is
  sensitive to the partial-ness specifically rather than to uniqueness in general. Worth
  recording that this mutation also wrote the WRONG index into the local database via
  `syncIndexes`; the source was restored, the suite re-run to rebuild the index, and the live
  shape re-verified.
  **A real defect fixed in the core-domain purity scanner.** It matched emitted JavaScript for
  `from '<specifier>'` with comments intact, so an ordinary English doc comment — distinguishing
  `"already granted"` from `"the write failed"` — was read as an import and failed the suite.
  Building with `--removeComments` fixes the whole class: a runtime import is code, and code is
  never a comment, so stripping cannot hide a violation. **Negative-controlled after the fix:**
  a contracts value import that is genuinely USED fails, while an `import type` still passes,
  which is exactly the G-CORE-CONTRACTS lock. My first control run reported a miss and was
  wrong — an unused import is erased by TypeScript, so it is not a runtime import at all.
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 RUN_DB_IT=1` adapter suite **100/100** ·
  `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** (contracts 154, http-transport 157, core-domain 87,

  with 0 probe rows · all five lint guards OK · `pnpm format:check` clean. `pnpm typecheck`
  fails ONLY on `developer-portal-storybook`, the same `@core/*` alias failure recorded above.
  **Not wired into the API.** No module provides these repositories and no route consumes

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5c.1 — registry write/assign actions, and an honest escape
  hatch for codes the server needs before the UI does.**
  **The problem was one I created in 5a.** `check-permission-registry` holds the registry and
  the admin navigation identical in BOTH directions, which is right — a code neither side uses
  is either a retired feature or a screen nobody gated. It also blocks every API surface built
  before its UI, which is the normal order of work here. 5c.3–5c.5 need codes the navigation
  has never had, so the rule had to grow a deliberate exception before it blocked real work.
  **Seven codes added, 26 → 33**, across two new resources (`permission`, `user_role`) and
  four new actions (`update`, `destroy`, `assign`, `revoke`): `permission.index`,
  `role.create`, `role.destroy`, `role.update`, `user.update`, `user_role.assign`,
  `user_role.revoke`. Sorted with the same `localeCompare` the guard checks against, which is
  why `user_role.*` precedes `user.create`.
  **`assign` and `revoke` are separate actions, not one `manage`.** Granting authority and
  removing it carry opposite risks: the dangerous direction is upward, and
  no-delegation-above-self constrains only granting. An operator who may offboard someone need
  not be one who may promote them. For the same reason assignment is its own resource rather
  than part of `user.update` — changing a display name and changing what somebody may do are
  not the same risk.
  **No `user.destroy`**, deliberately: accounts are deactivated, never deleted, because an
  audit trail that can lose its subject is not an audit trail.
  **The escape hatch is a map, not a list.** `SERVER_ONLY_PERMISSION_CODES` requires a REASON
  per code, so "why can nothing reach this?" is answered in the file rather than in someone's
  memory, and an abandoned entry stays visible in review. The guard now enforces four further
  rules: a server-only entry must name a real code, must carry a substantive reason, must not
  ALSO be in the menu (that is a contradiction — the UI reaches it), and every registry code
  must be reachable from the menu or declared here.
  **Two defects in my own guard, both found by negative-controlling it rather than by a green
  run.** (1) The server-only parser used an entry-terminator lookahead and silently dropped
  the LAST entry, which surfaced as the final code looking undeclared; it now slices between
  key positions. (2) The "has a reason" check tested that the value STARTED with a quote
  followed by a non-space character — which `''` satisfies, because the closing quote is
  itself non-space. The empty-reason rule therefore never fired. It now counts letters, which
  also survives Prettier splitting a long reason across concatenated lines. **7/7 rules caught
  after the fixes**, including both regressions, with the admin menu verified untouched.
  **An old claim of mine is no longer true.** `pnpm typecheck` is now **15/15 successful,
  0 cached** and `pnpm lint` **17/17** — the `developer-portal-storybook` `@core/*` failure I
  reported under 3c.5, 4b, 4c, 4d, 4e, 5a and 5b is gone. `--traceResolution` shows

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5c.2 — effective permissions resolved from active
  assignments, and the administrator system role.** 5b's repositories are now reachable from
  the API.
  **Union, not fallback — a correction to the plan.** The sub-pass was scoped as "assignments
  with `user.permissions` as fallback". Written that way it is NOT behaviour-preserving:
  preferring assignments once any exist would silently revoke the embedded grants the moment
  somebody received their first assignment. `resolveEffectivePermissions` therefore returns
  the UNION, so an assignment can only ever ADD authority. The embedded array is transitional
  and retires when assignments are the only writer.
  **The tier ceiling is the escalation guard.** A role contributes nothing if its `baseRole`
  sits above the holder's own coarse role. Demoting somebody from `admin` to `staff` while an
  admin-tier assignment survives would otherwise restore their authority in full, and the
  demotion would look applied while changing nothing. The hierarchy lives in one table because
  `'admin' < 'customer' < 'staff'` alphabetically — a string comparison would invert the check
  while still looking like one, and there is a test pinning exactly that.
  **Resolution is conditional, and that is a cost decision.** The guard resolves effective
  permissions ONLY when a route actually requires one. No route does today, so the common
  authenticated path must not have grown two queries; a test asserts neither repository is
  touched when nothing is required.
  **Other properties encoded:** revoked assignments are filtered in the resolver as well as in
  the repository query, so an audit view or a test fixture passing every assignment cannot
  accidentally grant revoked authority; a dangling assignment whose role was deleted grants
  nothing rather than failing the request, because failing would lock people out.
  **The administrator system role is seeded, and it is the only one.** Composing a `staff`
  role is a business decision, and seeding a guess would make it look ratified.
  `administrator` is the only role whose content is not a judgement call — it holds every
  registry code by definition, so it cannot fall behind as the registry grows — and it is
  `isSystem`, which the repository refuses to update or delete.
  **Seeding is an operator action, not a bootstrap step**
  (`pnpm --filter @saha-textile/adapters-db-mongo seed:system-roles`). A write on every process
  start races when instances start together and would let a registry change reach production
  without anybody deciding to apply it. Verified idempotent against the live database: the
  second run reported `updated` rather than `created`, one role, **33** permissions, with
  `createdAt` preserved and `updatedAt` moved.
  **Negative-controlled, four mutations, all caught:** removing the tier ceiling, replacing the
  union with assignments-only, honouring revoked assignments, and making resolution eager. The
  last is what proves the cost claim rather than asserting it.
  **A defect the tests could not see.** The guard-test stub `vi.fn(async () => [])` infers
  `never[]`, so mocking an assignment failed `tsc` while vitest ran it happily — 103 tests
  green and typecheck red. Typed explicitly; recorded because a suite passing is not the same
  as the suite being sound.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** — core-domain **87 → 98**,
  api **97 → 103**, adapter **100 → 103** (contracts 157, http-transport 157, storefront 57,
  admin 61) · `MONGODB_PORT=27018 RUN_DB_IT=1` adapter suite **103/103** · `pnpm typecheck`
  **15/15** · `pnpm lint` with all five guards OK · `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm
e2e:session-rotation` **12/12** with 0 probe rows, which is also the proof the new DI
  providers resolve in the real application · `pnpm format:check` clean.

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5c.3 — the `/admin/roles` surface, first to be
  deny-by-default in this API.** Five routes, each demanding a specific permission.
  **Deny-by-default is proven on the wire, not asserted.** A new e2e check seeds a real
  administrator — `role: 'admin'`, correct audience, valid session, and an EMPTY grant list —
  and requires **403**. The `admin` role alone opens nothing, which is the whole point of the
  registry. Granting the seeded administrator role then returns **200 with no re-login**,
  because the guard resolves effective permissions per request rather than trusting what the
  token was minted with. Anonymous gets 401, and a storefront session gets 401 rather than 403
  — the audience boundary answers before any permission question is asked.
  **Negative-controlled:** removing `@RequirePermissions` from the list route fails the check
  with `an ungranted admin was allowed in: 200`, then restored and verified by `git diff`.
  **These routes could be added safely precisely because they are new.** The lockout hazard
  raised in 5a was about adding requirements to EXISTING routes; a new surface nobody can reach
  yet refuses nothing that worked before.
  **`@Audience('admin')` is not redundant beside the permission checks.** A storefront session
  that somehow held an administrative grant must still be unable to reach this surface: the
  audience boundary is about which browser context a cookie was minted for, not about what its
  holder may do.
  **The invalidation rule has two halves, and both are tested.** Changing a role's permissions
  bumps `permissionsVersion` for every LIVE holder, or their sessions keep running on the
  authority they held a moment ago. A label-only edit bumps NOBODY — forcing everyone through a
  token rotation for a cosmetic rename would be its own defect. Re-sending the same codes in a
  different order counts as no change. Deletion invalidates holders too, and does so FIRST: a
  dangling assignment already resolves to nothing, but a live session keeps its minted version
  until something forces a re-derive.
  **A partial-failure decision worth naming.** Bumps are per user, and a single failure is
  logged rather than thrown. Abandoning midway would leave the role changed with some holders
  invalidated and others not, and no record of which. The residual risk is bounded and strictly
  better than a half-applied write.
  **`key` and `baseRole` are immutable, and `isSystem` cannot come from a request.** Editing
  `baseRole` would silently re-rank everyone already holding the role, which is an escalation
  dressed as an edit; accepting `isSystem` would let a caller mint a role the administration
  surface then refuses to delete. System roles are refused with a stated 409 rather than the
  repository's silent null, so an operator learns why instead of watching a role appear to
  vanish on save.
  **Contract and route counts moved together:** 49 → **54 operations across 47 paths**, and
  `check-route-contracts` independently reports **54 routes across 12 controllers**, auth
  family 25 → 30.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** with api **103 → 115** ·
  e2e **13/13** with 14 probe accounts and **0** remaining · `generate:openapi` green ·
  `pnpm typecheck` **15/15** · `pnpm lint` **17/17** with all five guards · `pnpm format:check`
  clean.
  **Formatting note, discovered while writing this entry.** An inline code span containing a
  double asterisk collides with bold emphasis in Prettier's MDX parser: it strips the
  surrounding spaces and the list indentation, which is what corrupted this entry when first
  written. Route globs are therefore written without it here.
- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5c.4 — the `/admin/users` surface: role grant and revoke,
  no-delegation-above-self, last-administrator protection, and audited offboarding.**
  **No delegation above self is TWO conditions, and both are needed.** The role's `baseRole`
  must sit within the actor's own tier, AND the role's permissions must be a SUBSET of what the
  actor effectively holds. Tier alone would let a staff administrator hand out a staff-tier
  role carrying permissions they do not have themselves — authority created from nothing.
  Subset alone would let somebody grant an admin-tier role whenever its permission list
  happened to be narrow, and tier is what governs what a holder may later be granted. Together,
  nobody can create authority they do not already possess, which is what stops a compromised
  mid-level account bootstrapping itself upward.
  **The refusal does not name the missing permissions.** Echoing them would turn a 403 into a
  map of the permission space for anyone probing it. Asserted in the unit suite and on the
  wire.
  **Last-administrator protection covers both ways out.** Revoking the final live admin-tier
  assignment is refused, and so is disabling the last account holding one — otherwise the
  surface that manages authority can remove the last authority able to manage it, and with no
  first-administrator bootstrap (6a) nobody could get back in without direct database access.
  The count is over ASSIGNMENTS, the authoritative model going forward; during the migration an
  account could still hold power through embedded `permissions`, so this can refuse a
  revocation that would in fact have been survivable. **Refusing too often is the safe
  direction for this particular check**, and that is a deliberate choice rather than an
  oversight.
  **Offboarding revokes sessions outright, not just the token version.** `SessionGuard` already
  refuses a non-active account on the next request, so the bump alone would be correct — but
  revoking kills the refresh tokens too, so an offboarded operator's stolen cookie cannot be
  replayed against a system that has merely not re-read their status yet. Re-enabling revokes
  nothing, and an actor cannot disable their OWN account: it is the one action from which they
  could not recover through this surface.
  **`deleted` is not an offerable status.** Offboarding is `disabled`: the row survives so the
  audit trail keeps its subject, and the email stays claimed rather than becoming
  re-registerable by somebody else. `setStatus` was added to `AuthUserRepository` for this.
  **Grants name only a role, never permissions.** Letting a caller attach permissions to a
  grant would reintroduce the free-form privilege strings the registry abolished, and would
  make two users holding the same role mean different things.
  **Proven on the wire, then negative-controlled.** A new e2e seeds a LIMITED administrator
  (may assign roles, holds little else), has it create a role carrying `role.destroy`, and
  requires **403** when it tries to grant it; requires 403 on granting the admin-tier system
  role; and requires **409** when revoking the only administrator assignment. Removing each
  protection in turn fails that check with the exact symptom —
  `privilege escalation was allowed: 201` and `the last administrator was revocable: 200` —
  then restored and re-run.
  **A test defect worth recording.** An assignment fixture omitted `revokedAt`, and
  `undefined !== null` reads as revoked, so the resolver correctly ignored it and the
  expectation failed. The fixture was wrong, not the resolver: treating a missing field as
  revoked is failing CLOSED, which is the direction that check should be wrong in.
  **Counts moved together again:** 54 → **58 operations across 51 paths**, and
  `check-route-contracts` independently reports **58 routes across 13 controllers**.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** with api **115 → 131**
  (contracts 157, http-transport 157, core-domain 98, storefront 57, admin 61) · adapter
  **103/103** with `RUN_DB_IT=1` · e2e **14/14** with 16 probe accounts and **0** remaining ·
  `generate:openapi` green · `pnpm typecheck` **15/15** · `pnpm lint` **17/17** with all five
  guards · `pnpm format:check` clean.
  **Next executable step:** pass 5c.5 — a read-only permission-registry listing for the grant
  UI. Then 5c.6, the security-negative campaign. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5c.5 — the permission registry published for the grant
  UI.** One read-only route, `GET /admin/permissions`, gated on `permission.index`.
  **Read-only, deliberately.** The registry is a compile-time closed set, not data. A route
  that could add a code would defeat the reason it is closed: an unknown permission would
  become grantable again and `RequirePermissions` would stop being a compile-time check. Adding
  a permission stays a code change plus the route that consumes it.
  **`serverOnly` is published rather than filtered out.** Seven codes have no admin navigation
  entry yet. Hiding them would be worse than showing them apart: the codes ARE grantable, so a
  screen that cannot display what was granted is a screen that disagrees with the server.
  **No service class.** The answer is a pure function over a constant; a class that only
  forwarded to it would be indirection with nothing to hide.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** with contracts **157 →
