- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 5c.6 — the security-negative campaign.** Six new e2e
  checks, each written to FAIL if its control were removed rather than to describe it. Angular
  route guards are usability only; every case calls the endpoint directly. **e2e 14 → 20.**
  **Direct API.** All ten admin routes are swept for an authenticated administrator with an
  EMPTY grant list and each must answer **403**. A 404 or 400 would mean the handler ran and
  the authorization gate did not, so the assertion is on 403 specifically rather than on any
  error.
  **Vertical escalation.** A storefront customer session reaches no admin surface, and is
  answered **401 rather than 403** — the audience boundary replies before any permission
  question, so a customer cannot even learn the surface exists.
  **BOLA.** One customer cannot read another's order by guessing an identifier.
  **Self-escalation.** Granting to SELF is the shortest escalation path; an operator holding
  `user_role.assign` still cannot give themselves the administrator role, because the subset
  rule closes it.
  **Bulk mixed-target.** A role carrying one permission the actor holds and one it does not is
  refused WHOLE, and the target is then re-read to prove nothing was partially applied — a
  half-applied grant would leave somebody holding authority nobody decided to give them.
  **TOCTOU and multi-tab.** Two live sessions for one account, authority revoked underneath
  both, and the very next request on EACH must already be refused — no re-login, no token
  change, no cache expiry. This is what proves the guard re-derives permissions per request
  rather than trusting what the token was minted with.
  **Two harness defects found by trying to break the campaign rather than by running it.**
  **First: my negative control lied.** Three mutation runs reported MISSED — including the
  TOCTOU case, the most valuable one. The Mongo container had stopped, so the e2e never
  executed, and the harness read "no failures" as "the control did not bite". The mutation
  runner now refuses to interpret a run that produced no tally at all. With that fixed and both
  revocation filters removed, the check fails exactly as intended:
  `first tab kept revoked authority: 200`.
  **Second: a failed run poisoned the next one.** Each check cleans up its own rows at the end
  of its body, which an assertion throw skips. Aborted runs left 2 roles and 3 assignments in
  the e2e database, and the following runs failed at 19/20 for reasons unrelated to the code —
  last-admin protection tripping on a stale assignment. RBAC rows are now cleared
  unconditionally in the global teardown, the same hygiene the rate-limit counters already had.
  Confirmed stable at 20/20 across three consecutive runs afterwards.
  **One mutation was absorbed rather than caught, and that is a good outcome.** Swapping the
  guard's `listActiveForUser` for `listAllForUser` changed nothing, because
  `resolveEffectivePermissions` filters revoked assignments itself. Both layers had to be
  defeated before the TOCTOU check failed — which is the defence in depth 5c.2 was written for,
  demonstrated rather than asserted.

  **Pass 5c is complete**, and with it pass 5. Next executable step: pass 6a —

  impossible to re-run. It is what every admin browser E2E has been owed since Pass 2, and it

  **A second, independent cause of the intermittent 19/20.** Debris was real, but not the whole

- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 6a — first-administrator bootstrap.** An operator CLI, not
  a route. **e2e 20 → 22.**
  **Why not a route.** Every other way in requires an administrator to already exist — invites
  are admin-only, role assignment is deny-by-default — which is correct and leaves exactly one
  hole: the first one. A public endpoint to fill it would be an unauthenticated route that
  mints total authority, guarded by nothing but the hope it is disabled in production. This
  needs a shell on the machine and the database credentials, a boundary the network cannot
  cross.
  **Impossible to re-run, not merely idempotent.** It refuses whenever administrative authority
  already exists — a live admin-tier assignment, OR any account carrying the coarse `admin`
  role. The second invocation is a refusal with a non-zero exit, never a quiet no-op that mints
  a second unaccounted administrator.
  **The password is generated, never supplied.** Taking one as an argument would put it in
  shell history, the process table and quite possibly a CI log. 24 CSPRNG bytes as `base64url`,
  printed once, stored only as an argon2 hash — and the e2e asserts it never reaches the audit
  entry.
  **Audited at `critical` with `actorUserId: null`.** Nobody granted this; the operator
  bootstrapped it. Recording the new account as its own grantor would fabricate a delegation
  that never happened.
  **Proven as the operator would run it, not only as a function.**
  `pnpm --filter @saha-textile/api bootstrap:first-admin -- --email …` created the
  administrator, a second run refused, and that account then signed in against the LIVE API on
  port 4000 and reached both `/admin/roles` and `/admin/permissions` — the deny-by-default
  surfaces from 5c.3 and 5c.5.
  **A vulnerability I reported to myself, and then disproved.** A new check asserted that
  registration ignores an injected `role`, and it FAILED with
  `registration honoured an injected role: admin`. Before claiming a privilege-escalation hole
  I probed a clean database directly: the handler hard-codes `role: 'customer'` and the created
  account is a customer. The assertion had queried `emailNormalized`, which registration does
  not reliably set, so it matched a DIFFERENT account — the bootstrapped administrator. The
  check now filters on `email` and re-asserts the document's own address before reading its
  role. **No vulnerability existed.**
  **Ordering turned out to be part of the test.** The bootstrap check first sat at the end of
  the suite and failed on its own success criterion: every earlier check seeds an administrator
  directly, which is exactly the state it must refuse. It now runs FIRST, which is also what a
  bootstrap means.
  **Two refusal paths, and the overlap had to be engineered around.** Removing either check
  alone still leaves the bootstrap refusing, so neither was individually provable through the
  happy path. The suite now constructs the one state where only the holder check applies — an
  admin-tier assignment held by an account that is not itself `role: 'admin'`. **All five
  controls now bite:** holders refusal, account refusal, both together, the audit write, and
  the role grant.
  **A third harness leak, caught by the assertion added earlier today.** The refusal assertions
  call the bootstrap with addresses they expect to be REJECTED, so those are never registered
  for cleanup — and when a refusal genuinely fails, the account it should not have created
  survives into the next run and breaks it for an unrelated reason. Users are now cleared
  wholesale in teardown rather than by tracked id or address; tracking every address a check
  might create is a losing game in a database that exists only for this suite. Stable at
  **22/22 across five consecutive runs**.
  **And a defect in my own mutation runner.** One mutation broke the build; the runner
  correctly refused to interpret a run that never happened, then exited WITHOUT restoring the
  source. Restoration now sits in a `finally`.
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **22/22**, e2e
  database verified empty by enumeration · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** ·
  `pnpm typecheck` **15/15** · `pnpm lint` **17/17** · `pnpm format:check` clean · the CLI
  exercised for real in both directions.
  **Local environment.** `saha_textile_local` now holds the bootstrapped administrator
  `owner@saha-textile.test` and the `administrator` system role. The password was printed to
  this session's transcript, so it should be treated as disclosed and changed before that
  database is ever used for anything real.
  **This unblocks the admin browser E2E owed since Pass 2**, and removes the seeded-probe
  workaround the RBAC suites rely on. Next executable step: pass 6b — admin Security Settings
  with recent-password proof and PIN configure/change. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Admin browser E2E — the run owed since Pass 2, finally possible.**
  Driven against the real admin app on 4300 and the real API on 4000, signed in as the
  administrator that 6a's CLI created. No seeded probe anywhere in it.
  **Session and storage.** The login screen renders at `/auth/login`; `localStorage` holds only
  `setting_store` with **no token and no JWT-shaped value anywhere**, and `st_csrf` is the only
  readable cookie — the historic admin defect confirmed gone against a real browser. Password
  login through the FORM reaches `/dashboard`, a reload survives on cookies alone, and the
  dashboard renders fully.
  **The 5c surfaces, from a browser session rather than `inject()`.** `/admin/roles`,
  `/admin/permissions` and `/auth/admin/me` all answer **200**; the authority endpoint reports
  the `administrator` role and **33** effective permissions.
  **Last-administrator protection in a real browser: 409.** Revoking one's own administrator
  role is refused with `The request conflicts with the current state.`
  **The full write path, with CSRF.** Create a role → grant it to self → read it back → revoke
  → delete, ending **201 / 201 / 200 / 204**, with the authority endpoint showing the role
  appear and disappear.
  **4b and 4c proven end to end by accident, which is the best kind.** Granting a role to
  oneself bumps `permissionsVersion`, so the very next call returned **401 with
  `reason: permissions_changed`** — the 4b sub-code, produced by a real grant rather than a
  fixture — and rotating recovered it exactly as 4c specifies.
  **Expired-access recovery, twice over.** With the API restarted at `JWT_ACCESS_TTL=5s`: a
  genuine expiry answered **401 `session_expired`**, rotation returned 200 and the replay 200.
  Then the same thing UNATTENDED — the Angular interceptor did `/me` 401 →
  `POST /auth/admin/refresh` 200 → `/me` 200 by itself, landing on `/dashboard` with storage
  still clean.
  **Logout through the MENU, which Pass 3c.5 recorded as unverified.** Hovering the profile
  menu and clicking Log out opens a confirmation modal; confirming redirects to `/auth/login`
  and `/me` answers **401 `session_missing`** — not `session_expired`, so the session is
  genuinely gone rather than merely aged. A signed-out visit to `/dashboard` redirects to a
  rendered login screen, and `/admin/roles` answers 401.
  **Two false alarms I raised and disproved before reporting them as defects.** A bodyless
  `DELETE` carrying `content-type: application/json` returned **400** rather than 409; sending
  it the way a browser naturally would gives 409, so the 400 was my own call — worth noting
  that a client which sets that header on a bodyless DELETE gets a confusing 400, but not a
  defect in this pass. And the Log out anchor appeared to be a dead control with no attributes
  and no effect from `.click()`; the template shows
  `(click)="confirmationModal.openModal('')"`, so it opens a modal rather than logging out
  directly. **Checking the source beat reporting the symptom.**
  **Local database after the run:** 1 user, 1 role, 2 assignment rows of which 1 is active, 5
  audit events — the grant/revoke history from the browser cycle kept as designed, nothing
  orphaned.
  **Still owed:** PIN login was not exercised, because the bootstrapped administrator has no
  PIN and setting one is 6b. The 15-minute idle soft lock is 6c and does not exist yet. Tool:
  claude-code.
- 2026-08-08 — [DONE] **Auth Pass 6b — admin Security Settings API: credential state, PIN
  lifecycle, password change.** **e2e 22 → 24.**
  **What already existed, and what did not.** `POST /auth/admin/pin` already required password
  proof on every change, which is the strictest reading of recent-password proof. Missing were
  the READ surface a screen needs, PIN REMOVAL, and password CHANGE — a self-service credential
  path distinct from the emailed-token reset.
  **`GET /auth/admin/security` reports state, never the credential.** `hasPin` rather than the
  hash, so a screen can render change-versus-set; the two suspension states kept separate
  because they behave differently and an operator must be told which they are in —
  `pinLockedUntil` is the five-failure lock and self-clears, `pinRevalidationRequiredAt`
  follows a privileged reset and clears only when the new password is used. Plus a live session
  COUNT, deliberately not the rows: handing a controller session documents puts device
  fingerprints and token hashes one careless serialization from a screen.
  **PIN removal is a POST, not a DELETE**, because it carries the password proof and a bodyless
  DELETE cannot. It also resets the preferred method to `password` in the same operation — left
  on `pin`, an operator would meet a PIN screen for a credential that no longer exists, a login
  they can neither complete nor explain.
  **Password change ends every session, including the one that made it.** Signing the operator
  out of their own browser is the intended cost; the alternative leaves whoever else was signed
  in exactly where they were. The PIN is deliberately NOT suspended — that is the reset path
  answering a suspected compromise, and a voluntary change by somebody who just proved the old
  password is not the same event.

  Removing `revokeAllForUser` from the password change left the suite at **24/24**.
  `setPasswordHash` bumps `tokenVersion` in the same write, so ACCESS tokens die either way and
  an assertion that only checks for 401 passes under both implementations — while the REFRESH
  row survives, meaning a stolen cookie could rotate straight back into a live session and
  outlive the credential it was obtained under. The check now requires the refresh itself to
  fail, and the mutation is caught. **4/4 controls bite:** proof removed, sessions not revoked,
  preferred method left on `pin`, and `hasPin` fabricated.
  **Proven as credentials, not as fields.** The e2e sets a PIN and then signs in with it,
  removes it and confirms the same PIN no longer authenticates, and confirms the old password
  stops working while the new one starts.

  probe accounts, 0 remaining and the database verified empty · `pnpm turbo run test --force`

- 2026-08-11 — [DONE] **Weak-PIN policy in core-domain.** **e2e 24 → 25**, core-domain
  **98 → 134**, api **131 → 138**.
  **What was actually missing.** `AdminPin` in contracts was a six-digit regex and nothing
  else, and its own comment had named core-domain as the home of denylist/sequential/repeated
  rejection since the schema was written. No such module existed, so `000000` and `123456`
  were acceptable administrator PINs. The owner lock pairs the PIN with a five-failure account
  lock precisely because a million values is a small keyspace; that lock is what stops online
  guessing, and this policy removes what a guesser would try inside those five attempts.
  **Three rules, and what each is worth.** REPEAT is written as "a unit whose length divides
  six", so it catches `777777`, `121212` and `123123` in one rule rather than only
  six-identical-digits — **1,090** PINs. SEQUENTIAL is a ±1 run counting around from 9 to 0;
  the wraparound is deliberate, because `890123` is one finger motion exactly like `123456`
  and treating the 9→0 boundary as a defence would draw a line the keypad does not have —
  **20** PINs. The DENYLIST is **38** curated entries for what no structural rule catches:
  keypad columns and diagonals, grouped pairs and triples, famous constants, round numbers.
  Total **1,148 of 1,000,000**, about one in 871, and that number is asserted rather than
  described — the policy's justification fails in both directions if nobody knows it.
  **A test that earned its place before the code was finished.** A denylist entry a structural
  rule already refuses is dead weight that reads as coverage, so the suite refuses one. It
  immediately caught `696969` — `69` typed three times, already a repeat — and the same
  reasoning removed `420420`. The two most-cited weak PINs are structurally weak; listing them
  by name would only have made the list look longer.
  **Deliberately NOT rejected: date-shaped PINs.** `DDMMYY`/`MMDDYY`/a doubled year are, in
  every published analysis, the largest weak class. Rejecting all valid dates costs ~3.6% of
  the keyspace, and more to the point the owner locked three named rules; a structural fourth
  is the owner's decision, not this file's. Recorded in the module as a known omission with the
  reasoning, so a later reader raises it as a question rather than rediscovering it as a gap.
  **Two enforcement points, and the ordering at each is the interesting part.** In
  `AdminSecurityService.setPin` the strength check runs BEFORE the recent-password proof: the
  verdict is a fact about a string the caller just typed and reveals nothing about the account,
  while Argon2 is deliberately expensive, so a caller cannot spend our CPU by submitting
  `000000` in a loop. In `AdminInviteService.accept` it runs BEFORE `consume` — `consume` is
  atomic and single-use, so refusing afterwards would spend the invitation on a failed request
  and leave an invitee unable to accept because somebody typed a sequence.
  **Why the refusal is shaped as a validation issue.** `HttpExceptionFilter` replaces every
  message with a safe one chosen by status, so `BadRequestException('that PIN is sequential')`
  reaches the operator as "The request could not be processed" — true, useless, guaranteed
  support ticket. `issues` is the one channel the filter preserves, deliberately, because
  issues describe the caller's own input. A weak-PIN refusal is exactly that, so it travels as
  `validation_failed` with a stable `pin_sequential` / `pin_repeated` / `pin_denylisted` /
  `pin_shape` code a screen can localize. Saying this much is safe because both surfaces are
  AUTHORIZED — a live admin session, or possession of a single-use invite token. It changes
  nothing at login, where a credential refusal stays the bare `unauthorized` envelope.
  **Negative-controlled, five ways.** Removing the check from `setPin` → 3 unit failures;
  removing it from invite acceptance → the non-consumption check fails; making the sequential
  rule never fire → 12 failures; narrowing the repeat rule to six-identical-digits → 5
  failures, including the keyspace count. Then the `setPin` mutation was replayed against the
  E2E, which failed with `weak PIN 123456 was accepted: 204` at **24/25**. Every source was
  restored from a byte-comparison backup and re-verified green.
  **Commands actually run:** `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** (contracts 160,
  http-transport 157, core-domain **134**, api **138**, storefront 57, admin 61) · adapter
  **103/103** with `RUN_DB_IT=1` on port 27018 · `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation`
  **25/25**, 29 probe accounts, **0** remaining, 7 audit rows removed, database verified empty
  by enumeration · `generate:openapi` green at **62 operations across 55 paths** — unchanged,
  correctly, because this pass adds no route · `pnpm typecheck` **15/15** · `pnpm lint`
  **17/17** with all five guards · `pnpm format:check` clean · `git diff --check` clean.
  **Still owed, unchanged by this pass.** The Security Settings SCREEN does not exist, so the
  PIN half of the admin browser E2E remains owed. The E2E covers the refusal at the Security
  Settings surface only; invite acceptance with a weak PIN is proven at the service, not over
  HTTP. And the PASSWORD denylist is still missing — `Password` in contracts is `min(12)` with
  a comment promising a core-domain common-password check that, exactly like the PIN one until
  today, does not exist. Next executable step: the admin Security Settings screen, which now
  has a policy to surface. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-11 — [DONE] **A failed recent-password proof answers 403, not 401.** **e2e 25 → 26.**
  Found while wiring the Security Settings gateway, which is why the screen is not in this
  entry: the defect had to be closed before a screen could call these endpoints.
  **The defect.** `requireRecentPasswordProof` threw `UnauthorizedException`, and both Angular
  interceptors read a 401 as "this session is gone". On a credential-classified route they
  call `onSessionLost()` at once; on any other route they rotate, replay, receive the same
  refusal and call it anyway. `onSessionLost()` runs `AuthStore.clear()` → `applyAnonymous()`,
  and `AuthGuard` then bounces the next navigation to `/auth/login`. So a MISTYPED CURRENT
  PASSWORD on the Security Settings form would have signed the operator out of a session the
  server still considers perfectly alive. No route classification avoids it — both branches
  reach the same call, because the status code was carrying a meaning the client could not
  help but act on.
  **Why 403 is the correction rather than a workaround.** The locked rule is `401` for an
  absent or invalid session and `403` for a caller who is authenticated but insufficiently
  authorized. Somebody reaching that line HAS a valid admin session — the global guard proved
  it — and has failed a step-up for one operation. That is the second case. The 401 was
  arguably always a violation of the rule; the client damage is what made it visible.
  Owner-decided 2026-08-11 after the alternative (a third client-side route classification)
  was put alongside it.
  **Not a blanket downgrade, and the E2E says so.** The new check asserts 403 with code
  `forbidden` and NO `reason` field on all three proof endpoints — a session-bound reason here
  would be read by both interceptors as a verdict on the session, and there is no session
  problem to report — then proves the session still works afterwards, and then proves the same
  three routes still answer **401** when there is no session at all. The 401 raised inside
  `settings()` for a missing account is untouched: that one really is an absent session.
  **Negative-controlled.** Reverting the throw to `UnauthorizedException` fails three E2E
  checks at **23/26**, naming `/auth/admin/pin answered 401 for a failed proof`. Source
  restored from a byte-comparison backup and re-verified.
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **26/26**, 30
  probe accounts, 0 remaining, database verified empty · `pnpm turbo run test --force`
  **11/11** (contracts 160, http-transport 157, core-domain 134, api 138, storefront 57,
  admin 61) · `generate:openapi` green at **62 operations across 55 paths** · `pnpm typecheck`
  **15/15** · `pnpm lint` **17/17** · `pnpm format:check` clean.
  **A gate worth knowing about.** `pnpm e2e:session-rotation` at the ROOT runs
  `turbo run build --filter=@saha-textile/api` first; the same script inside `apps/api` does
  NOT. Run the root one, or the suite silently tests a stale `dist`.

  every time, whether the custom work was requested or merely follows from another task — hand
  the owner bullets, a wireframe, the conditional logic, a reuse inventory and an explicit

  **The handoff protocol.** `session-handoff-protocol.mdx` is a tool-agnostic, domain-neutral
  standing instruction for generating a complete handoff at the end of any session — admin,
  storefront, API, database, deployment, testing or portal alike. It exists because the owner
  was re-typing the same context-transfer request every time a thread switched, and a
  hand-written request necessarily under-specifies. It mandates deriving the handoff from
  re-read documents and inspected source rather than recall, and requires a mandatory
  corrections-and-wrong-turns section on the grounds that a handoff reading as an unbroken
  series of successes has been edited for presentation. Tool: claude-code.

- 2026-08-11 — [DONE] **Password change no longer locks the operator out of their own browser.**
  The defect the previous entry found and deliberately left, fixed where it diagnosed it: in the
  response.
  **The fix is three tokens.** `changePassword` now takes `@Res({ passthrough: true })` and
  calls `this.sessions.clearCookies(reply)` after the service call — which is exactly what
  `resetPassword`, TWO METHODS BELOW IT in the same controller, has always done. That proximity
  is why the omission survived review: both methods call the same `revokeAllForUser`, and only
  one of them finished the job.
  **Revoking server-side was only half the operation.** Without clearing the reply cookies the
  browser keeps its httpOnly `st_access`/`st_refresh`, now pointing at sessions that no longer
  exist. `CsrfGuard` then behaves exactly as designed and the combination wedges the tab: it
  enforces only when a session cookie is PRESENT — a stale one is present — and then fails
  closed because no live session resolves. So the next unsafe request, the LOGIN ITSELF
  included, is refused **403 before any credential is read**, which is why `failedLoginAttempts`
  stays at zero while the operator watches a correct password be rejected. The guard is not the
  bug.
  **Audited rather than assumed.** Every `revokeAllForUser` call site was checked for the same
  omission: storefront password reset, admin password reset and the disabled-user path inside
  rotation all already pair with `clearCookies`, and `admin-users.service` offboards a DIFFERENT
  user, where there are no cookies on this reply to clear and not clearing is correct. This was
  the only unpaired one.
  **Why the E2E never caught it, which is the more useful lesson.** Both existing post-change
  login assertions build a FRESH `CookieJar`. The defect only exists when the browser still
  holds the cookies the change was made with, so a fresh jar could never see it — the suite was
  asserting the credential changed, not that the operator could still use it. The check now
  asserts the changing tab's own jar retains no `st_access`/`st_refresh`, and that signing back
  in from that same jar answers **200** rather than 403.
  **Negative-controlled twice, because the first control proved the wrong half.** Removing
  `clearCookies` fails at **25/26**, naming the assertion about session cookies left in the
  browser — but that one fires first, so the lock-out itself stayed unproven. A second mutation
  removed the fix AND the cookie assertion, and the run then failed on the assertion about the
  changing tab being locked out by CSRF failing closed on a stale cookie, confirming the 403
  symptom independently rather than inheriting it from the previous session's diagnosis. Both
  sources restored from byte-comparison backups and re-verified.

  accounts, 0 remaining, database verified empty · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11**

- 2026-08-11 — [DONE] **Weak-password policy in core-domain.** **e2e 26 → 27**, core-domain
  **134 → 163**, api **138 → 146**. The gap the PIN had this morning, closed on the credential
  that protects more.
  **What was missing.** `Password` in contracts was `z.string().min(12).max(256)` with a comment
  promising a core-domain common-password denylist that did not exist — the identical situation
  the PIN was in. The owner lock is twelve characters plus a denylist, for both audiences; only
  the first half was real, so `passwordpassword` and `SahaTextile2026` were acceptable.
  **Why the denylist looks nothing like a leaked-password list.** The length floor has already
  done most of that work: `123456`, `password`, `qwerty` — the entire head of every such list is
  under twelve characters and cannot be submitted at all. Porting one verbatim would add a few
  hundred entries that can never match, which reads as coverage and provides none. What actually
  survives the floor is a common word DECORATED until it is long enough, so the list stores BASE
  TERMS and the comparison normalises the candidate down to its base — lower-cased, stripped to
  letters and digits, then stripped of a trailing digit run. `Password1234` and
  `p-a-s-s-w-o-r-d-1` both reduce to `password`. Brand and locality terms are in the list
  deliberately: an operator reaches for the business name first.
  **One structural rule beyond the literal lock**, flagged rather than slipped in: `repeated`
  refuses a short unit typed three or more times, because a twelve-character floor accepts
  `aaaaaaaaaaaa` and `abcabcabcabc` and no denylist of WORDS will ever catch them.
  **Deliberately NOT done:** comparing the password against the user's own email, username or
  name. Genuinely valuable — `priya@example.com` passes everything here — but it needs the
  identity threaded into all five call sites, and it is not part of the lock. Recorded as a known
  omission in the module. Breached-password lookup stays a seam, as the build prompt says.
  **Enforced at all five surfaces, and the ordering at each is the interesting part.** Invite
  acceptance and password RESET check before their single-use token is consumed — refusing after
  would strand somebody who by definition cannot sign in, holding a spent link. Admin password
  change checks before the Argon2 proof, the same economy as `setPin`. And **storefront
  registration checks before the rate limit AND before the existence lookup, which is
  anti-enumeration rather than tidiness**: that handler answers a generic `401` for an address
  that already exists, so judging the password afterwards would make the same weak password
  answer `400` for an unknown address and `401` for a known one — turning any weak password into
  a probe that confirms an account exists.
  **Two test failures worth keeping.** The reachability test found `0123456789` UNREACHABLE:
  every submittable password that could normalise onto a short all-digit entry must be padded
  with digits, and the normaliser strips exactly those, so nothing can ever match it. Replaced
  with a twelve-digit entry. And a cap test written as `'q7'.repeat(128)` failed because that is
  a two-character unit repeated 128 times and the repeat rule refuses it, correctly — the test
  was wrong, not the code.
  **Negative-controlled, including the ordering claim.** Moving the registration check to AFTER
  the existence lookup fails the anti-enumeration assertion at **26/27**, so that ordering is
  pinned rather than merely commented. Removing the check from `completePasswordReset` fails two
  unit tests. Sources restored from byte-comparison backups.

- 2026-08-11 — [DONE] **Bootstrap password asserted against the policy, and one owed item
  disproved rather than fixed.**
  **The assertion**, added inside `generatePassword()` in `first-admin.ts` so it travels with
  the thing it guards. Honest about its worth: twenty-four CSPRNG bytes CANNOT fail
  `evaluatePassword` today — far past the floor, no repeating unit, cannot normalise onto a
  denylisted word — so it will not fire. It earns its place only as a tripwire: if somebody
  later shortens the token or swaps in a memorable-words generator, the account with the most
  authority in the system stops being the one account exempt from the policy every other
  account is held to. The failure path never echoes the password.
  **`setPinHash` leaving a stale `pinRevalidationRequiredAt` is NOT REACHABLE.** Carried as an
  owed item since 6b and traced today rather than fixed. The fact is true — `setPinHash` clears
  `failedPinAttempts` and `pinLockedUntil` and not the revalidation flag. The scenario is not:
  only `recordSuccessfulLogin` clears that flag and it runs on every password login for both
  audiences; a privileged reset sets the flag AND revokes every admin session; PIN login is
  refused while the flag is set, because `verifyAdminPin` returns `revalidation_required`
  before it consults the lock or the hash. So reaching Security Settings after a reset requires
  a PASSWORD login, which clears the flag, and no HTTP path arrives at PIN removal with it
  still set. Recorded as an unenforced invariant rather than a defect — worth tidying if
  `setPinHash` ever gains a second caller, not worth a pass now. **The brief's "everything
  owed" list is corrected accordingly**, so the next session does not spend an afternoon
  chasing a bug that cannot happen.

- 2026-08-12 — [DONE] **The audit trail is readable: `audit.index` and `GET /admin/audit-logs`.**
  **e2e 27 → 28**, contracts **160 → 168**, registry **33 → 34 codes**, OpenAPI **62 → 63
  operations across 56 paths**, route families **62 → 63 routes across 15 controllers**.
  **The gap.** Every admin mutation has been writing audit rows under a seven-year retention
  tier since the first RBAC pass, and nothing could read them. `AuditLogListQuery` had been
  documented in contracts as `GET /admin/audit-logs` and `AuditLogRepository.list` had been
  implemented in the Mongo adapter — only the route was missing, so the trail was write-only
  and the question it exists to answer had no answer short of a database shell.
  **`audit.index` is its own permission, and that is the design decision.** Folding it into
  `user.index` or `setting.index` would have been less work and wrong: this trail records what
  every OTHER operator did, so being able to administer a resource must not imply being able to
  read the history of everyone who has touched it. Same reasoning that keeps `user_role.assign`
  and `user_role.revoke` apart. Declared server-only with that justification, since no screen
  gates it yet. Owner-approved before the registry was touched — 5a locked it as a closed set.
  **The response is the entity MINUS its two hashes**, and the reason is specific rather than
  hygienic. The build prompt's rule is that internal schemas may carry hashes and public ones
  never can, and `ipHash` is the case that shows why: IPv4 has under 2^32 values, so an
  unsalted digest of an address is reversible by exhaustive search in seconds — publishing it
  would be publishing the address that storing a hash was meant to avoid. `toAuditLogEntry`
  lives beside the schema so the omission is performed in the one place it is explained; a
  controller doing it inline is a controller where the next field added to `AuditLog` silently
  becomes public. `diffs` and `metadata` are safe by construction, redacted before they are
  ever written, so the read model does not re-litigate that.
  **`page`/`pageSize` needed `z.coerce`**, because the schema validates a QUERY STRING where
  every value arrives as text — without it `?page=2` fails as "expected number, received
  string" and the endpoint is unusable from a browser. Coercion does not loosen the 200 cap,
  which is what stops this being an unbounded export of the security trail, and the suite
  asserts the cap holds for `201` and `'201'` alike.
  **Read-only, permanently.** No write, update or delete route, and there should never be one:
  rows are appended in the same transaction as the mutation they describe and removed only by
  the retention job, by tier and by age. A route that could edit an audit row would remove the
  only property that makes the trail evidence rather than a log file.
  **Negative-controlled three ways**, one per property. Making the projection a cast instead of
  a destructure fails BOTH the contracts test and the E2E, on the assertion that the read model
  never publishes a hash. Re-gating the route on `user.index` fails the separation-of-authority
  assertion. Dropping the 200 cap is caught by the oversized-page assertion. Sources restored from
  byte-comparison backups and re-verified.
  **Two wrong guesses of mine, both caught by the check rather than by review.** The probe role
  was created with `{ code, name }` when the contract is `{ key, label, baseRole }` — 400. Then
  the trail was searched for `action: 'role.create'` when `RolesService` writes
  `admin.role.create`; the row was there all along, and the audit count rising from 7 to 8
  proved it before the assertion did.

  accounts, 0 remaining, database verified empty · `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11**

  `generate:openapi` green at **63 operations across 56 paths** · `check-permission-registry`
  **34 codes across 25 resources, 8 server-only** · `check-route-contracts` **63 routes across
  15 controllers** · `pnpm typecheck` **15/15** · `pnpm lint` **17/17** · `pnpm format:check`
  clean · `git diff --check` clean.

- 2026-08-12 — [DONE] **Session list and per-session revoke, both audiences.** **e2e 28 → 29**,
  OpenAPI **63 → 69 operations across 62 paths**, route families **63 → 69 routes**. The build
  prompt has required session list/revoke since D3; only a COUNT existed, which the Security
  Settings screen was already displaying — so the surface was visibly half-built.
  **Six routes, three per audience:** `GET /auth/{audience}/sessions`,
  `DELETE /auth/{audience}/sessions/:id`, `POST /auth/{audience}/sessions/revoke-others`. One
  service implementation serves both, because ownership is the same rule either way.
  **The read model is a strict subtraction**, and every omission is deliberate.
  `refreshTokenHash`, `previousRefreshTokenHash` and `csrfSecretHash` are credential material:
  a device list that leaked them would publish the fingerprints reuse detection is built on,
  making the list more dangerous than having none. `device.userAgentHash` and `device.ipHash`
  go for the reason the audit read model drops the same pair — an unsalted digest of an IPv4
  address falls to exhaustive search in seconds, so publishing the hash is publishing the
  address. What remains is what a person needs in order to recognise a session and decide
  whether to end it: label, country, created, last seen, idle expiry, absolute expiry, and
  `current`. Without `current` a device list is indistinguishable rows and the owner cannot
  tell which one signing out ends the page they are looking at.
  **Ownership answers 404, never 403**, which is the locked rule: a 403 confirms the id exists
  and turns the endpoint into a probe for other people's session ids. The same 404 covers an
  unknown id, an already-revoked one, and one belonging to the caller's OTHER audience — and
  the E2E asserts the stranger's session is still alive afterwards, so the refusal is proven to
  be a refusal rather than a silent success.
  **Scoped by audience as well as by user.** The same human may hold a storefront session and
  an admin session; showing one surface's devices on the other would leak the existence of
  back-office access to a shop account with no business knowing about it.
  **Revoking the CURRENT session clears this browser's cookies** in the same response — the
  lesson from the password-change lockout earlier today, applied before it could bite again.
  **`revoke-others` deliberately spares the caller.** It is the "somebody else is signed in as
  me" control, so signing the person out of the device they trust while the suspected intruder
  is elsewhere would be exactly backwards.
  **No new permission code, and no new revoke reason.** These are self-service over the
  caller's own sessions, so they need no grant; `logout` and `logout_all` already existed in
  `SessionRevokeReason`. Force-revoking ANOTHER operator stays out of scope — that needs its
  own code and its own lockout analysis.
  **Negative-controlled three ways.** Dropping the `session.userId === principal.userId` clause
  fails with a stranger's session answering 204 instead of 404. Spreading the whole session
  into the summary fails on the list publishing `refreshTokenHash`. Making `revoke-others`
  revoke everything fails the surviving-session assertion. Sources restored from
  byte-comparison backups.

- 2026-08-12 — [DONE] **The last role-gated route converted — and it was hiding an
  audience-confusion hole.** **e2e 29 → 30**, registry **34 → 35 codes**. Set out to close a
  deny-by-default gap; found a genuine cross-audience defect on the way, and a 500 beside it.
  **THE HOLE, found by probing rather than by reading.** `PATCH /orders/:id/status` carried
  `@RequireRoles('admin','staff')` and NO audience. `SessionGuard` enforces an audience only
  when one is DECLARED, and `OrdersController` declares none — correctly, since its other
  routes are a customer reading and placing their own orders. So a staff member who also shops
  on the storefront could reach the back-office route with their STOREFRONT cookie: the role
  check passed and the handler RAN. A throwaway probe against the real application returned a
  **500 from `MongoOrderRepository.updateStatus`**, which is how far the request got — with a
  real order id it would have changed the order's status. This is precisely the
  "storefront cookie against admin endpoint" case the security matrix requires to be
  impossible.
  **`@Audience('admin')` is declared on the ROUTE, not the controller**, because the other
  three routes must stay reachable from the storefront. The refusal is 401 rather than 403, so
  a storefront session cannot even learn the surface exists.
  **`order.update` replaces the coarse role.** The last route in the codebase authorizing by
  role alone; `admin` must not silently mean every capability, and a fulfilment operator may
  need to advance an order without being able to read or raise one. Declared server-only —
  the navigation gates `order.index` and `order.create` only.
  **THE LOCKOUT ANALYSIS the brief asked for, and it is not a formality.** `administrator`
  holds `[...PERMISSION_CODES]`, but that grant is written by `ensureSystemRoles`, which is
  DELIBERATELY not run at boot. So any role row seeded before this registry addition — every
  existing deployment — does not hold `order.update` until an operator re-runs
  `seed:system-roles`. **Whoever could change order status yesterday cannot today until that
  seed runs.** Recorded in the route's own doc comment as an operator note, not only here.
  **A second defect fixed in passing:** a missing order surfaced as **500**, because the
  repository throws a bare `Error` and nothing mapped it. An operator mistyping an order
  reference was told the server had broken. Now a 404, narrowly — any OTHER repository failure
  still propagates as a 500, because a write that failed for an unanticipated reason must not
  be reported as "no such order".
  **Negative-controlled three ways, and one control taught something.** Removing `@Audience`
  fails with a storefront cookie reaching the route — but with **403**, not 200, because the
  permission gate catches it too. The two fixes are independent controls and the run proves
  both are load-bearing: a granted staff member would still have crossed audiences with only
  one of them. Reverting to `@RequireRoles` fails both the new check and the existing
  every-admin-route-refuses-an-ungranted-administrator check at 28/30. Dropping the 404
  mapping fails with `an unknown order answered 500, not 404`. Sources restored from
  byte-comparison backups.
  **Commands actually run:** `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation` **30/30** ·
  `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** (contracts 168, http-transport 157, core-domain 163,
  api 146, storefront 57, admin 69) · `check-permission-registry` **35 codes across 25
  resources, 9 server-only** · `generate:openapi` **69 operations across 62 paths**, unchanged
  because no route was added · `pnpm typecheck` **15/15** · `pnpm lint` **17/17** ·
  `pnpm format:check` clean.
  **Deny-by-default is now complete**: no route in the codebase authorizes by coarse role
  alone. **Worth a follow-up:** no guard asserts that, so a future controller could reintroduce
  one silently — a source-level check in `pnpm lint`, beside the permission-registry guard,
  would make the property permanent rather than currently-true. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-12 — [DONE] **`check-route-authorization.mjs` — deny-by-default is now enforced, not
  merely true.** Sixth source-level guard in `pnpm lint`, beside naming, browser-auth,
  i18n-parity, route-contracts and permission-registry.
  **Two rules, one defect each.** (1) A role-gated route must declare an `@Audience`, because
  `SessionGuard` enforces one only when declared — the exact shape of the order-status hole
  found hours earlier, where a staff member's STOREFRONT cookie satisfied a back-office route.
  (2) A route authorizing by role alone must be named in `ROLE_ONLY_ROUTES` with a reason, so
  coarse-role authorization cannot be reintroduced silently.
  **Twelve legitimate role-only routes, all self-service** — the caller's own PIN, password,
  credential state, identity, sessions and quick-resume. There is no other resource involved,
  so there is nothing to hold a permission against: the authority is "I am this person", which
  the session proved, plus "I am back-office staff", which the role states.
  **Three of the twelve are recorded as a KNOWN GAP rather than an endorsement.** The invite
  routes — create, list, revoke — are gated on the coarse `admin` role, so every administrator
  may issue invitations regardless of the permissions they hold, and invitation is the ENTIRE
  privilege-granting surface. No-delegation-above-self constrains what may be granted, which is
  why this is a gap and not a hole. Converting it needs its own pass, its own code and its own
  lockout analysis. Writing the justification down is what turns an invisible gap into a
  reviewed one.
  **A stale exemption is itself a failure.** An entry naming a route that no longer exists
  reads as a reviewed decision and would silently excuse a future route reusing the path.
  **Comment stripping is load-bearing, not tidiness:** `orders.controller.ts` carries a doc
  comment explaining why the route is no longer `@RequireRoles`, and matching it would make the
  guard report the very defect it documents as still present.
  **Negative-controlled three ways.** Reinstating the original defect fails BOTH rules by name.
  A comment mentioning `@RequireRoles` on a route that has none does not trip it. A stale
  allowlist entry fails. Sources restored from byte-comparison backups.

  role-only and justified, 18 public, every role-gated route audience-bound**, matching

  `pnpm turbo run test --force` **11/11** · `MONGODB_PORT=27018 pnpm e2e:session-rotation`
  **30/30** · `pnpm format:check` clean.
- 2026-08-12 — [N/A] **Account separation between the storefront and the admin: what is
  actually true.** Probed against the running application rather than inferred, because the
  owner asked and the answer is partly not what was hoped.
  **There is ONE `users` collection and ONE `User` contract**, with `role` as the
  discriminator. `collection-names.ts` declares `User: 'users'`; there is a single
  `user.model.ts`. `AdminUserProfile` is an admin-scope EXTRA (employee code, department,
  inviter), not a separate account. This is the locked design — the build prompt's target model
  lists `users` once, and the separation it calls for is of CREDENTIALS
  (`authIdentities`/`passwordCredentials`/`pinCredentials`, pass 7, not yet done), not of
  people.
  **Measured, on a real instance:** customer → admin login **401 blocked**; staff → admin login
  **200**; customer → storefront login **200**; **staff → STOREFRONT login 200 — ALLOWED**; and
  a staff member's storefront cookie against `/auth/admin/me` **401**, so session-level audience
  isolation holds.
  **So the two directions are not symmetric.** A customer can never obtain admin access, which
  matches the matrix's explicit exclusion. A staff or admin account CAN sign in to the
  storefront and shop, because storefront login checks only that the account is active. The
  matrix never forbade that direction — it says only "Customer accounts cannot log into admin"
  — so this is current design rather than a defect, but it is not what the owner expected and
  it is now an open question rather than an assumption. **No change made; awaiting a decision.**

- 2026-08-15 — [DONE] **Local development moved to HTTPS, deleting the development-only cookie
  model.** Groundwork for Chunk D OAuth (Meta refuses a plain-HTTP origin) but the real gain is
  independent of it. `isSecureContext()` returned `config.nodeEnv === 'production'`, so locally
  session cookies were **not** `Secure` and **not** `__Host-` prefixed while production used
  both — two security models, only one of them ever executed by a developer or a test. It is now
  `config.cookies.secure`, a `COOKIE_SECURE` **opt-OUT defaulting to `true`**, so a forgotten
  variable fails closed and turning it off is a visible diff. Verified live: the API now issues
  `__Host-st_access` and `__Host-st_refresh` (httpOnly + Secure) and `__Host-st_csrf` (readable,
  Secure) **on localhost**, and a full admin login → `/auth/admin/me` → `/admin/customers`
  round-trip returns 200 over TLS.
  **How.** `pnpm setup:local-https` (new) issues a certificate from the machine-local mkcert CA
  into a gitignored `certs/`; storefront and admin Vite servers and the API's Fastify listener
  read it, each degrading to HTTP when the pair is absent so a fresh clone still boots. The API
  reads `TLS_CERT_FILE`/`TLS_KEY_FILE` and never falls back to a generated certificate — serving
  TLS nobody verified would be worse than serving none. Deploys leave both unset; Nginx
  terminates TLS there and the API speaks HTTP behind it, which is why `COOKIE_SECURE` is
  deliberately NOT inferred from whether this process terminates TLS.
  **Origins moved with it** so nothing broke: `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, both apps' `config.json` +
  `config.example.json`, both `runtime-config.ts` defaults, both legacy `environment.ts` objects,
  and `.claude/launch.json` (which also gained `autoPort: false` on api/admin/storefront — all
  three are origin-pinned by CORS, runtime config and, soon, the provider consoles, so a
  wandering port is a broken app). `apps/api/.env.example` documents the pair.
  **Two test-suite consequences, both fixed by testing the new truth rather than avoiding it.**
  `csrf.test.ts` asserted "Secure in production only"; it now asserts Secure-by-default in every
  environment, the explicit `COOKIE_SECURE=false` opt-out, and that the string `'false'` parses
  as false (`z.coerce.boolean()` would make it truthy — an operator disabling the flag would
  have enabled it). And `session-rotation.mjs` hardcoded `st_access`/`st_refresh`/`st_csrf` in 22
  places, so it stopped finding its own cookies; it now derives them from the server's own
  `cookieNames()`, which means **the E2E exercises the `__Host-` production spelling for the
  first time**.
  **Also in this pass, per owner decision:** the admin mock customer service is DELETED rather
  than renamed — `user.service.ts` and `user.queries.ts` are gone and the Points page reads the
  live `injectAdminCustomersQuery`, the same query the Customer Ledger beside it already used.
  The owner's objection to `MockCustomerService` was correct: a name only defensible because the
  code is temporary is an argument for removing the code. The orphaned `IUserModel` went with it.
  **Locale correction, made permanent.** `AGENTS.md` claimed "runtime en↔bn" and three config
  files advertised `supportedLocales: ["en","bn"]` while the app ships `en.json` + `fr.json`
  with `availableLangs: ['en','fr']`. That stale claim is why agents keep "fixing" French into
  Bengali. All four corrected to en+fr, each carrying the reason: **Bengali is added once
  development is complete**, deliberately, so two live locales keep the i18n seams exercised and
  the third is a data change rather than an architecture change.
  **Gates:** typecheck **17/17** · unit suites all pass (contracts 168, http-transport 157,
  core-domain 163, api **182**, storefront 57, admin 72, msg91 3) · DB-IT **108/108** ·
  session-rotation E2E **34/34 exit 0** · seven lint guards OK, `turbo run lint` 0 errors ·
  `format:check` clean · OpenAPI 88 operations / 75 paths · both Angular apps build · all three
  servers verified serving trusted TLS (`curl` without `-k`).
  **Owed, and now twice-proven:** the DB-IT suite runs against `saha_textile_local` and its

  operator login**. It did so twice in this session alone, each time requiring a re-bootstrap

  `bootstrap:first-admin` after any `RUN_DB_IT=1` run. Also unresolved: `set:local-credentials`

  and Facebook-without-email (B) are owner decisions still under discussion, and no OAuth code
  was written. Tool: claude-code.
- 2026-08-16 — [DONE] **Chunk D storefront identity — the whole BACKEND, verified-before-creation.**
  Locked first as `DEC-SIGNUP-VERIFICATION` after a long design review in which the owner's
  design was criticised, corrected in both directions, and several of my own claims withdrawn.
  Everything below is API and below; no storefront UI was written.
  **The idea the whole tranche enforces:** a customer row is created only after email AND phone
  are OTP-verified, so there is no unverified-account state — which removes the pre-hijack
  takeover class outright. An attacker cannot squat an address they do not control and wait for
  the real owner to be handed their account.
  **`POST /auth/storefront/register` is DELETED**, not deprecated. It created an account from an
  email and a password with no verification; shipping the verified flow beside it would have
  left the unverified door open next to the locked one, and an attacker uses whichever opens.
  **Nine new routes:** `signup/{start,field,otp/request,otp/verify,finalise}`,
  `oauth/{state,google,facebook}`, `oauth/{connect,disconnect}`, plus `login-methods` and
  `step-up/request`. 99 routes total, 99 OpenAPI operations across 86 paths.
  **Contracts (`signup.ts`, `oauth.ts`).** `FinaliseSignupRequest` accepts ONLY an optional
  password — a test feeds it `email`, `phone` and `providerSubject` and asserts they are
  stripped, so at the one moment an account is minted the request physically cannot name an
  identifier. Thirteen stable refusal codes, pinned.
  **Core.** `OAuthVerifierPort` (opaque string in, settled identity out — no SDK types, no raw
  payloads) and `signup-policy.ts` as pure functions. `mayDiscloseExistence` takes ONLY
  `proofOfControl`: not whether a password exists, not which providers are linked, because
  varying the answer on those leaks the account's shape to whoever asks. `credentialsAfterRemoval`
  counts a verified email or phone AS a credential, which is what makes disconnect safe for a
  social signup with no password. Runtime purity (`G-CORE-CONTRACTS`) still holds.
  **New package `packages/adapters-auth`** — `google-auth-library@11.0.2` pinned. The library
  checks signature/iss/aud/exp; the adapter checks what it deliberately does not: the **nonce**
  we minted (a token captured from another session passes every other check), `email_verified`,
  and subject presence. An unverified address is DROPPED rather than carried with a false flag.
  Facebook uses no SDK — Graph `debug_token` with a server-built app access token, checking
  `is_valid` (catches revocation, which no expiry check would), `app_id` matching ours (the
  substituted-app attack), expiry and `user_id`. **`emailVerified` is unconditionally false**
  from the Meta adapter even when an address returns: Meta asserts nothing we can check, and
  making it conditional would push a judgement call into every caller.
  **Persistence.** `pendingSignups` — sliding 15m TTL capped at 30m absolute, unique on
  `sessionKey` so a second tab JOINS a signup rather than racing it, and **deliberately no
  unique index on email or phone**: reserving addresses would let one caller park thousands in
  fifteen-minute blocks, a denial of service dressed as a safety feature. `authIdentities` port
    - adapter (the model existed; nothing could reach it). `findByPhone` added to the customer
      port — phone is a login credential now.
      **Anti-enumeration, moved to where it belongs.** Nothing is revealed at signup start or OTP
      request. Disclosure happens only AFTER proof of control, where it is safe and lets the flow
      offer "sign in instead" rather than failing at the end. Delivery is enqueued so a
      non-existent account spends no credits AND the response time is identical — skipping the send
      synchronously would leak in the timing dimension what the message no longer leaks in words.
      **Owner decisions recorded in the lock**, including two where the owner overruled me: phone
      OTP is a full login equivalent (SIM-recycling risk accepted and written down), and the
      `UserStatus` split into `CustomerStatus`/`AdminUserStatus` — which turned out BETTER than my
      recommendation, since operators genuinely have four states to a customer's five.
      **Contract simplified mid-build:** the specified `stepUpToken` was dropped. The step-up code
      is presented directly on connect/disconnect; an intermediate token needed its own storage,
      expiry and single-use rule to carry a proof across one extra round trip, and removing it
      removed the replay window it would have opened.
      **E2E rewritten** to walk the real flow (start → verify email → verify phone → finalise). The
      code cannot be read back — challenges store an HMAC under a server pepper — so the harness
      overwrites the stored hash with one it chose, using the same pepper. Everything downstream is
      genuine; only the secret the test cannot know is substituted. **34 → 35 checks.** The weak
      password case now proves the ORDERING (a rejected password leaves the proof intact and a good
      one immediately after still completes), and a new check proves an OTP request answers
      identically for known and unknown addresses.
      **Four bugs found by the tests, three of them mine.** (1) The pending-signup upsert put `_id`
      in `$set`, which Mongo rejects as immutable — a first signup succeeded and every RESTART
      failed, i.e. "signup works, but not if you abandon and retry". (2) A `beforeEach` arrow
      returned `mockReset()`'s value; Vitest treats a returned FUNCTION as a teardown hook and kept
      invoking a throwing stand-in. (3) A stub hash of `h(${value})` embedded its input, so
      "the row contains no plaintext" could never fail however the service behaved — replaced with
      sha256. (4) The E2E teardown left `pendingSignups` behind and its own leftovers assertion
      caught it.
      **Gates:** typecheck **19/19** · contracts 176 · core-domain 177 · **adapters-auth 23** ·
      http-transport 157 · api **197** · storefront 57 · admin 72 · msg91 3 · DB-IT **116/116** ·
      session-rotation E2E **35/35 exit 0** · seven lint guards OK, `turbo run lint` **0 errors** ·
      `format:check` clean · OpenAPI 99 operations / 86 paths, all ids unique.
      **Owed:** MSG91 `template_id` vs `flow_id` — both are sent with the same value until a real
      account confirms which the endpoint honours; OTP template ids are placeholders. Storefront UI
      (four approved screens), gateway, auth store and en/fr copy are NOT started. Tool: claude-code.

## Developer portal / docs (`apps/developer-portal`, `docs/`)

> ### ⚠ OWED: two collections are missing from the ratified Schema Nebula graph
>
> **`productQuestions` and `ratingAggregates` are real, durable, business-meaningful
> collections that belong in the 65-node graph and are not in it.** They are implemented and
> in use; the graph simply never caught up. This is a documentation debt, not a design
> choice.
>
> They currently sit in `NON_GRAPH_COLLECTIONS` so the `collection-names.test.ts` assertion
> passes, which is exactly what makes the debt easy to forget — an unpaid reconciliation and
> a deliberate exclusion looked identical in one list. As of 2026-08-16 they no longer do:
> `collection-names.ts` separates **`GRAPH_RECONCILIATION_OWED`** (this debt, which may only
> ever shrink) from **`RUNTIME_ONLY_COLLECTIONS`** (`authRateLimits`, `pendingSignups` —
> machinery with a TTL, correctly outside the data model and never to be added to it).
>
> **Clearing it means ADDING two nodes to `docs/_data/instruments/schema-nebula.json`,
> taking it 65 → 67, and removing both names from `GRAPH_RECONCILIATION_OWED`.** It does not
> mean renaming a collection to fit the graph, and it does not mean deleting the entry.
> Until then the governed diagram is knowingly incomplete, and anyone reading it as the whole
> database is being misled by two rows.
